When New Hope CORPS lost federal funds in 2017 (before she was Mayor), Levine Cava was a Commissioner representing South Dade and **advocated for the County to find funds to help New Hope**, given its importance in her district【62†L43-L45】.

Thus, one could say Levine Cava **helped shield New Hope from closure**, but this was a policy choice to aid a service provider, not an illicit act.

No evidence suggests she or her office benefited personally from that – it was about continuity of care for the homeless/addicted population. - **Ethical Conduct:** Levine Cava’s administration has emphasized ethics and transparency (she created a chief integrity officer position at the County).

The allegation, detailed in a lawsuit and ethics complaint, is that she **manipulated a technicality** to appear first on the 2024 mayoral ballot by dropping part of her last name【74†L83-L92】【74†L118-L126】.

Florida law requires candidates to use their legal name or a known nickname.

Levine Cava’s legal last name is “Levine Cava,” but by submitting one form with that and a later form with just “Cava,” she ensured being listed alphabetically at “C”.

The complaint claims this was intentional fraud.

This matter is pending; if the COE or courts find she willfully falsified an oath, it could be a legal and ethical violation.

We found **no direct claims that Mayor Levine Cava protected anyone involved in homeless program misconduct.** In fact, there’s an example to the contrary: In September 2023, Mayor Levine Cava formally asked State Attorney Rundle to investigate reports of **elder abuse in public housing** (in a Miami Herald exposé about a county housing facility)【72†L15-L19】.

But regarding the Homeless Trust or New Hope, there’s no sign she ever impeded an investigation.

If anything, as noted, she has worked with Rundle on task forces to target fraud (e.g., real-estate fraud against seniors)【76†L181-L189】【76†L203-L211】.

Unless evidence emerges that she, for example, told the State Attorney to “go easy” on someone (and we found none), Levine Cava does not appear to be part of any conspiracy of protectionism. - **Nepotism/Employment:** A quick note – Levine Cava herself has been accused informally of nepotism in one instance: hiring her daughter’s law firm for county lobbying in 2021.

However, that was reviewed and it turned out the daughter was a junior attorney at a firm the County hired, not a direct contract with the daughter.

It caused a brief media stir but no ethics violation was found.

In the arena of homelessness/housing funds, she seems to have acted to **support and expand funding** (e.g., backing the Trust’s purchase of hotels for housing, supporting “Homekey” style programs, etc.) rather than colluding to misdirect funds.

No concrete evidence ties her to misconduct in these programs in the 2010–2025 timeframe based on our research. ## State Attorney Katherine Fernandez Rundle – Prosecution Decisions and “Protectionism” **Katherine Fernandez Rundle** has been the Miami-Dade State Attorney (top prosecutor) since 1993, giving her a pivotal role in deciding which public corruption or fraud cases to pursue.

More germane to our topic, **no charges were brought in various county corruption allegations** (outside of homelessness) during her tenure – critics say she is **too lenient on the “political elite.”** In 2024, the Miami Herald described her facing mounting criticism for how her office handled several cases, noting she **“deflected criticisms”** and stayed unchallenged in elections【54†L5-L9】.

The **Florida Association of Criminal Defense Lawyers (FACDL)** took the extraordinary step in April 2024 of writing a public letter demanding an independent *“ethics review”* of Rundle’s office for a *“win-at-all-costs”* culture and prosecutorial misconduct【74†L177-L184】.

They alleged some prosecutors under her watch **bullied defense attorneys and withheld evidence**, which are serious ethical breaches.

While this speaks to **internal ethics**, it also indirectly supports the idea that *justice hasn’t always been even-handed* in Miami.

One could argue that if, hypothetically, someone in the Homeless Trust or County was misusing funds but was politically connected, Rundle’s history suggests she might not prioritize that case.

Notably, **Ron Book** (Homeless Trust chair) is a powerful figure and close to many politicians; any serious investigation involving the Trust would be delicate.

We found no evidence Rundle ever had to consider charges against Book or anyone on the Trust board in the last 15 years. - **Organized Protectionism?:** The user asks about an “organized effort to shield perpetrators from prosecution.” In Miami-Dade, if such an effort exists, many point to **Rundle’s long tenure** as a linchpin.

She has been unopposed or easily re-elected for decades, possibly because the local political/legal establishment supports her staying in place.

One tangible example: in 2024, **Shlomo Danzinger**, a mayoral candidate, accused Rundle of secretly investigating him as retaliation because he called her out for *“reluctance to prosecute criminals”* and tried to get Governor DeSantis to remove her【74†L171-L179】.

He tied this to law enforcement frustrations as well.

But indeed, **Rundle has rarely prosecuted local officials for corruption**, despite Miami-Dade having a steady trickle of corruption scandals (many handled federally or by the County Inspector General instead).

This discrepancy is what fuels theories of *protectionism*. - **Positive Efforts:** On the other side of the ledger, Rundle has aggressively prosecuted fraud when it aligns with her initiatives.

A recent case in June 2021 involved her and Mayor Levine Cava announcing the takedown of a real estate fraud ring targeting an elderly owner – charges included grand theft, organized scheme to defraud, conspiracy, etc.【76†L181-L189】【76†L211-L219】.

That case shows she will prosecute *individual wrongdoing* in the homeless services realm when presented with evidence.

In sum, **State Attorney Rundle’s relevance** lies in the *expectation of skepticism*: If the user’s ongoing case involves pushing for accountability (perhaps trying to get a fraud prosecuted or an official charged), Rundle’s history suggests an uphill battle unless the evidence is ironclad.

Multiple sources note that she has been **Miami-Dade’s top prosecutor for three decades** and is deeply entrenched【56†L1-L9】.

The FACDL’s 2024 letter and other critiques【74†L177-L184】 show that even the legal community has serious concerns about her office’s ethics.

For the user’s purposes, it may be relevant that **defense attorneys and even some law enforcement personnel have openly questioned Rundle’s commitment to prosecuting public corruption**.

If alleging a “pattern of protectionism,” these are the kind of points one would raise – e.g., *“Even when allegations surface, they tend to die on the vine; the State Attorney has a record of inaction in politically sensitive cases, as noted by observers【74†L171-L179】.”* This is **circumstantial but credible** context to include.

Just be cautious: there’s no direct proof Rundle conspired to cover up a specific homeless-funds fraud.

The pattern is more about inertia and selectivity. ## Timeline of Key Events (2010–2025) - **2010:** Miami-Dade County contracts with homeless service providers (including New Hope C.O.R.P.S.) incorporate enhanced **Conflict of Interest and Nepotism** provisions.

Stephen Alvarez is already Executive Director of New Hope by this time and continues in that role through 2025【1†L1-L4】. - **2013:** *Watchdog Report* notes the Homeless Trust’s HUD funding had grown to ~$29 million annually but warns of a **$1.1 million federal cut** looming【40†L1-L7】.

This is the context in which providers like New Hope feel pressure on funding streams. - **2014–2016:** Daniella Levine Cava is elected to the County Commission (District 8, including Homestead) and becomes involved in homelessness and housing issues from a policy side.

Katherine Fernandez Rundle wins re-election as State Attorney, continuing her long tenure without opposition.

In 2015, Rundle’s office closes an investigation into county fundraising irregularities with no charges – one of several instances that cause watchdog groups to question her diligence.

Meanwhile, New Hope C.O.R.P.S. expands services (e.g., outpatient programs) and likely hires Taylor Alvarez during this period (New Hope’s newsletters show Taylor in staff photos by 2016-2017). - **May 2017:** The **HUD OIG audit report** (2017-AT-1005) on the Homeless Trust is released.

The audit details are made public on May 23, 2017【16†L69-L77】.

Recommendations include reimbursing funds and tightening oversight.

Also in mid-2017, as a likely consequence of changing HUD priorities, **New Hope CORPS loses a renewal for transitional housing funding** (worth over $400K).

In **April 2017**, the Miami Herald reports that Miami-Dade County administration plans to dip into local reserves to keep New Hope’s rehab center open【62†L43-L45】.

Later that year, the County Commission approves an allocation (reports indicate they used part of the County’s opioid lawsuit settlement funds to cover approximately $400,000 for New Hope)【65†L4-L12】.

This event underscores a **turning point in funding strategy** – a move away from transitional housing towards Rapid Re-Housing (HUD’s new focus), which some providers like New Hope had to adapt to. - **February 2018:** A contentious **Homeless Trust Housing Subcommittee meeting** is scheduled to discuss the upcoming federal funding application (NOFA).

On Feb 15, 2018, Stephen Alvarez emails fellow providers about the Trust’s proposal to reallocate transitional treatment beds to a new Rapid Re-Housing program【11†L212-L220】.

Recognizing he’s a public board member, he seeks an ethics opinion (INQ 18-45) the same day to ensure any communication is Sunshine Law compliant【24†L71-L79】.

This is essentially a **policy fight**: providers vs.

Department of Justice regarding the treatment of homeless sex offenders (the County had a notorious encampment issue).

Separately, *Watchdog Report* in April 2019 mentions the Homeless Trust chair (Book) pushing for more funds and warns of **“need for transparency”**【40†L19-L27】.

The Commission on Ethics around this time provides training to Homeless Trust board members on conflicts of interest (per Ethics Commission annual reports).

Katherine Rundle is automatically re-elected State Attorney (no opponent) in August 2020 – a point noted by commentators who questioned the lack of challengers despite controversies【56†L7-L15】.

No allegations of misuse of the sudden influx of federal COVID/homeless funds emerge in Miami (in contrast to some other jurisdictions).

The Trust actually garnered praise for quickly sheltering homeless people during the pandemic. - **2021:** Mayor Levine Cava and State Attorney Rundle appear jointly to announce a **Senior Fraud Task Force** on June 18, 2021【76†L181-L189】.

Ethics-wise, the COE mediates some discussions.

By 2022, Rundle’s office charges that ex-employee, who goes to jail【49†L19-L27】.

This indicates **zero tolerance for frontline fraud** – a low-level crime was prosecuted fully, suggesting that when evidence is straightforward, action is taken. - **2022:** The Homeless Trust receives a **$5 million grant from Jeff Bezos’s Day 1 Families Fund** to fight family homelessness【20†L29-L35】, showing a big influx of private funding.

However, countywide, a major public housing scandal breaks (mismanagement at the Miami-Dade Public Housing agency, unrelated to the Trust).

Mayor Levine Cava fires the housing director and requests investigations – demonstrating her approach to **accountability when problems surface**.

This indirectly sets a precedent that she would likely act on any Homeless Trust corruption if it were clearly brought to her. - **2023:** **Mia Casa**, the Homeless Trust’s first housing project for homeless seniors, opens in March 2023【41†L11-L18】.

In mid-2023, a **Miami Herald series exposes neglect and abuse in some county-funded senior apartments**.

Although this is outside the Homeless Trust’s scope (it’s public housing for the elderly), Levine Cava formally asks State Atty.

For the Homeless Trust and its providers, 2023 is relatively quiet on scandal – the Trust did face **budget strains** as federal COVID housing funds expired, but the Mayor and Commission allocated local dollars to prevent a wave of evictions, working with the Trust.

In August 2024, Shlomo Danzinger (former Surfside Mayor, now a candidate for County Mayor) files a lawsuit and an ethics complaint alleging **Mayor Levine Cava committed election fraud** regarding her name on the ballot【74†L79-L88】【74†L118-L127】.

He also accuses State Attorney Rundle of **secretly investigating him and others** as political payback, and invokes Rundle’s *“apparent reluctance to prosecute criminals”* as a reason she should be removed【74†L171-L179】.

Publicly, **Rundle insists her office is ethical**, noting that one prosecutor at the center of misconduct allegations resigned in early 2024, and she set up a new **Prosecution Integrity Unit**【56†L11-L19】.

As for Levine Cava, the ballot name issue went before a judge in late 2024, who **dismissed Danzinger’s lawsuit** (reports say it was tossed on procedural grounds, effectively upholding Levine Cava’s ballot placement).

The COE’s inquiry into the ethics complaint is likely still ongoing or was rendered moot by the court decision.

This episode is a reminder of how **political tactics in Miami-Dade can involve accusations of fraud**, but such claims require substantial proof to succeed.

It underscores that **multiple reputable sources** (e.g., court filings, COE records) should be used when alleging wrongdoing, as politically motivated claims get scrutinized heavily. - **2025:** As of now, **Stephen Alvarez** continues to lead New Hope C.O.R.P.S., which is in the midst of expanding its residential facility (the Florida Legislature approved a local funding initiative for New Hope’s facility in FY2024-25)【57†L9-L17】. **Taylor Alvarez** remains a senior staff member, and New Hope maintains contracts with the Homeless Trust.

The **Miami-Dade Homeless Trust** is operating under a new federal funding cycle, emphasizing housing solutions like rapid rehousing and permanent supportive housing.

The Trust’s budget (over $40 million) is being closely watched due to the housing affordability crisis; no **funding scandals** have emerged. **Mayor Levine Cava** is running for re-election (assuming the 2024 election proceeds, she would be in a runoff if one occurs in late 2024/early 2025). **State Attorney Rundle** remains in office, having weathered the criticism; her next election is 2024, but as of our research, no serious challenger had filed, meaning she is likely to remain the SAO through 2025.

Provider Representative, Miami-Dade County Homeless Trust --- ### Re: Ongoing Concerns Regarding New Hope C.O.R.P.S. and Our Case Dear Mr.

For more than three years, we have been actively engaged with New Hope and its staff, consistently told that assistance was on the way, only to find ourselves repeatedly misled, abandoned, and ultimately subjected to actions that not only violated the mission of your organization but also contravened Florida law.

Our goal here is not to attack, but to present the facts as they unfolded—step by step—so you can see why we now request **immediate corrective action and emergency housing accommodations**. --- ### I.

Our Initial Contact with New Hope (December 2021 – January 2022) - In **December 2021**, Joshua Durden was assessed for housing by **Dee-Dee**, a staff member from New Hope.

When she finally resurfaced, she claimed that New Hope’s housing budget had run out but assured us new funding would come in **May 2022**.

He reviewed our paperwork and said we would need to wait for the release of **HUD Housing Choice Vouchers**, which we had already been told were expected around **October 1, 2022**. - Yet he refused to provide clarity, repeating vague statements and avoiding concrete commitments. - When Joshua sent him a thoughtful message of gratitude for his supposed efforts, Stephen did not reply.

She **denied ever hearing of us** and, most shockingly, claimed not to know what **Rapid Rehousing** even was—despite the fact that her **LinkedIn profile listed her as Senior Director of Homeless Services at New Hope**, overseeing both Rapid Rehousing and Housing Stability Vouchers. - When Janina pointed out this discrepancy, Taylor became hostile, accusing her of *“throwing a tantrum.”* Within an hour of us posting on New Hope’s public Facebook page about this contradiction, our comment was deleted.

A **sergeant from the Miami-Dade Police Department (MDPD)** arrived unannounced at our encampment behind a **Chase Bank property**.

Then, in a stunning violation of trust, he personally produced and posted a **fraudulent 5-day notice** on a private pole.

The ordinance cited applied only to **public property**, not the private land we occupied. - We pointed out, calmly and repeatedly, that Stephen had no legal authority to post or enforce such a notice—he was not a process server, a landlord, or law enforcement.

Together, they carried out what Florida law clearly defines as a **self-help eviction**: - Our belongings were **trashed, stolen, and deliberately destroyed**. - A locked chest belonging to Juan was taken by a man working with **Chase Bank**; when confronted, he resisted returning it. - Several of our pets—our companions and emotional lifelines—were lost or killed.

The cruelty of this act cannot be overstated. **New Hope, an accredited provider whose mission is to house and protect the unhoused, stood shoulder to shoulder with police to remove us from the only safe refuge we had left.** This was not negligence—it was **active collaboration in our erasure**. --- ### VI.

Pattern of Collusion and Systemic Failures Our case illustrates something larger than a series of isolated missteps: it reveals **a coordinated pattern of passive elimination**, where outreach workers under your leadership **withheld help, misled us, and ultimately partnered with law enforcement to clear us out**. - **Conflict of Interest:** Your dual role as **Executive Director of New Hope C.O.R.P.S.** and **Provider Representative on the Miami-Dade County Homeless Trust board** created an inherent conflict of interest.

The body tasked with oversight of New Hope was simultaneously influenced by its own executive leadership. - **Nepotism & Misrepresentation:** **Taylor Alvarez**, your niece, is listed as **Senior Director of Homeless Services at New Hope**, overseeing Rapid Rehousing and Stability Vouchers.

Yet when we called her in good faith, she denied knowing what Rapid Rehousing even was and accused Janina of “throwing a tantrum.” This was both dismissive and professionally inappropriate. - **Law Enforcement Collusion:** New Hope staff, particularly Stephen Heath, acted not as advocates but as **extensions of the Miami-Dade Police Department**, posting fraudulent eviction notices and participating in unlawful displacement actions.

Instead of protecting our rights, they **enabled police to carry out a self-help eviction** on private property, in direct violation of: - **F.S. § 83.67** – Prohibiting self-help evictions - **F.S. § 82.045** – Requiring civil court proceedings for unlawful detainer - **F.S. § 810.08** – Defining trespass only when proper, lawful notice is served All of these actions contradict **CARF accreditation standards**, which require ethical leadership, protection of client rights, transparency, accountability, and quality service delivery. --- ### VII.

The Unspoken Policy – “No Humans Involved” What has become clear is that our experience was not just the product of individual misconduct.

In our interactions, we observed repeated **spontaneous admissions** and **slips of the tongue** from both **New Hope staff** and **law enforcement officers**—remarks that revealed the underlying assumption: that because we were **homeless**, we were not entitled to the same protections or considerations as other residents.

This resonates disturbingly with the documented **“No Humans Involved” (NHI)** approach referenced in discussions of urban policy, where certain populations are quietly written off.

In our case: - We were told to wait months for housing that never came. - We were passed from one employee to another, each contradicting the last. - Our private health information was used against us. - Police and outreach workers coordinated to remove us from private land without due process. - Our pets—our only consistent companions—were lost and killed as a direct result of this action.

The **2010 directive by then-Governor Charlie Crist** added *homeless status* to Florida’s **hate crime guidelines**, requiring law enforcement to halt their own investigation and notify the State Attorney’s Office if bias against homeless individuals was even suspected.

The Human Cost of New Hope’s Failures For nearly **three years under New Hope’s watch**, our lives have been marked by cycles of hope and devastation: 1. **Dee-Dee’s false promises** (December 2021 – September 2022) – telling us of “the perfect place,” then ghosting us after more than **100 unanswered messages**. 2. **Adam’s brief honesty and disappearance** (mid-2022) – acknowledging we had been wronged, then vanishing after promising to help. 3. **Stephen Heath’s manipulation** (late 2022 – 2023) – shifting stories about income requirements, deflecting responsibility, and telling us *“Don’t thank me yet.”* 4. **Taylor Alvarez’s denial and hostility** (October 2023) – denying knowledge of Rapid Rehousing despite being publicly listed as responsible for it, then retaliating by deleting our attempt to ask for transparency online. 5. **The illegal eviction with MDPD and code compliance** (January 2024) – where our belongings were **trashed, stolen, and destroyed**, and our **pets were lost and killed**, under color of law but without legal authority.

These events did not just delay housing.

Instead of being lifted out of homelessness, we were **pushed deeper into it**. --- ### IX.

Our **service dog and pets**, essential to our emotional stability, require a safe indoor environment. 2. **Reassignment Within New Hope** - Reassign our case to a **different New Hope employee**—one without prior entanglement in the events we’ve described. - That employee should be required to **report directly to you weekly** on the progress of our housing case, so accountability is documented and visible. 3. **Commitment to Mission Integrity** - New Hope is accredited by **CARF International**, which requires upholding standards of ethical leadership, client-centered care, and protection of rights. - We ask that you review the documented transgressions of your staff—**Dee-Dee’s abandonment and privacy breach, Adam’s empty assurances, Stephen Heath’s illegal eviction actions, and Taylor Alvarez’s denial of her own role**—and take corrective action. --- ### X.

Under HB 1365, local governments (counties and municipalities) are prohibited from allowing people to regularly sleep or camp on public property, sidewalks, parks, or rights-of-way unless those public spaces are officially certified by the Department of Children and Families (DCF) for that use.

Designated spaces must meet specific standards — restrooms, running water, security, accessibility, and behavioral health services — and must also prohibit drug or alcohol use. - The law took effect **October 1, 2024**, and includes mechanisms for residents, business owners, or the state to sue jurisdictions that fail to comply after written notice. - While the law aims to address public safety and quality-of-life concerns, there is widespread concern (among homeless rights advocates, service providers, and people living unhoused) that the state did not simultaneously provide adequate shelter capacity, clear case management, or guaranteed access to the alternatives required by the law. - Local policy responses in Miami-Dade thus far include commitments to add shelter beds, a navigation center, and tiny-homes.

However, based on reports, implementation remains uneven and many individuals remain unsheltered or improperly housed. - Additionally, Miami-Dade has a large affordable housing gap (millions of dollars and thousands of units) which compounds the inability of outreach or provider organizations to place people even when the legal environment demands it. --- ## How This Supports / Reinforces Your Narrative Here are the ways this policy and recent data strengthen the arguments in your case: 1. **Show legal expectations and failures** The law (HB 1365) explicitly *requires* that jurisdictions provide alternatives (shelters, designated spaces) when prohibiting sleeping or camping.

In your case, New Hope staff repeatedly promised housing or assistance but failed to deliver.

This legal requirement undercuts excuses rooted in “lack of authority” or “lack of alternatives.” 2. **Demonstrate conflict between statute and action** When staff or policies allowed or participated in illegal eviction or posted notices on private property under false pretenses, that directly conflicts with the legal framework that protects due process—even under the stricter demands of HB 1365. 3. **Frame broken promises as more than mismanagement** Given the urgency under state law, delays, disappearances, and contradictory information (from Dee-Dee, Adam, Stephen Heath, Taylor Alvarez) are not just unfortunate—they violate the spirit of these laws and breach promised legal protections afforded to people experiencing homelessness. 4. **Highlight systemic risk of erasure** The policy landscape provides tools to enforce rights (lawsuits, inspections), but your lived experience shows how powers are being misused or allowed to slide.

These include laws banning camping or sleeping in public, aggressive encampment sweeps, fines and arrests for panhandling, and even **defunding housing services in favor of policing**.

This trend has accelerated amid rising visible homelessness – the U.S.

Department of Housing and Urban Development (HUD) estimated over **654,000 people were homeless on a single night in 2023, up 12% from the previous year**【1†L166-L170】.

Supreme Court decision (*Johnson v.

Boise*) **removed federal barriers to enforcing anti-camping laws**, spurring a wave of new local ordinances imposing fines and jail time on unhoused individuals【1†L78-L87】【19†L109-L118】.

Many of these efforts trace back to model legislation by the **Cicero Institute**, a Texas-based think tank: bills in numerous states have been introduced or passed to **mandate enforcement of camping bans and redirect funding from “Housing First” programs to short-term shelters or treatment**【24†L58-L66】【24†L147-L155】.

Below is a state-by-state summary of key actions (2022–2025) targeting unhoused people.

Each state section lists recent **state laws or proposals** and notable **local policies/ordinances**, along with responsible agencies or actors.

Overall, the trend shows a **rise in punitive approaches** – especially in Republican-led states adopting Cicero’s model laws – often in the absence of adequate housing alternatives.

Citations to source material and legislation are provided. ## Alabama - **Panhandling Laws Struck Down:** Alabama had state laws criminalizing panhandling and “loitering” for begging, but in 2022 a federal court **permanently enjoined** their enforcement, ruling such bans unconstitutional under the First Amendment【31†L11-L18】. (The 11th Circuit upheld this in 2023, affirming that soliciting charity is protected speech.) State officials have since fought to revive or replace these laws【31†L15-L23】.

Civil rights groups (including the **ACLU and NLCHP**) have opposed these efforts as end-runs around the court rulings【31†L19-L27】. - **Local Median Bans:** Several Alabama cities have considered or passed ordinances banning pedestrians (and thus panhandlers) from standing on street medians, citing traffic safety.

While no major Alabama city ban was reported as law by 2025, **Mobile and Montgomery** debated median panhandling bans.

Advocates argue such measures are really about making homelessness less visible, not safety, and they caution that First Amendment challenges are likely if enforced【34†L69-L77】【34†L119-L127】. ## Alaska - **Anchorage Camping Ordinances:** Alaska’s largest city, **Anchorage**, has grappled with large outdoor camps amid insufficient shelter space.

A May 2024 ordinance **prohibited camps within a half-mile of any shelter** and capped the size of camps (no more than 25 tents per site)【33†L359-L367】.

Mayor Bronson’s administration and the Anchorage Police have enforced illegal camping laws more vigorously when seasonal shelter was available (e.g. during winter).

The **Anchorage Police Department** created Homeless Outreach teams and often cited or moved people camping on city property once an alternate shelter or sanctioned site was (theoretically) offered【25†L5-L13】【25†L31-L39】.

However, due to shelter shortages, many camps reappeared elsewhere, leading observers to dub it a “whack-a-mole” battle【25†L41-L47】. ## Arizona - **Vetoed State Legislation:** In 2023, Arizona’s Republican-led legislature passed **Senate Bill 1413**, which would have *forced* cities to dismantle homeless encampments and charge people living in them with criminal trespass.

Gov. **Katie Hobbs** vetoed SB1413 in June 2023, criticizing it for “effectively criminaliz[ing] experiencing homelessness” without addressing root causes【18†L57-L65】【18†L71-L79】.

Justine Wadsack (R), sought to override local discretion by mandating camp clearances and punishments even when no shelter was available【18†L57-L65】.

She similarly vetoed SB1024 in 2023, which would have broadly banned tents/tarps in public areas (another anti-camping measure)【17†L5-L13】【17†L15-L18】. - **Encampment Injunction and “The Zone”:** Separately, Phoenix faced a court order regarding its large downtown encampment known as “The Zone.” In March 2023, a county judge declared The Zone a public nuisance and **ordered Phoenix to clear it**, following a lawsuit by local businesses【29†L149-L157】.

By mid-2023 the city, under court supervision, removed hundreds of people from The Zone encampment.

This clearance was touted by some (including Cicero Institute) as a success – reports claimed “80% of [Zone] residents have found more stable shelter” after the sweep【30†L259-L267】 – but advocates noted most were simply moved to other unsheltered locations or short-term motel stays.

Hobbs’ vetoes, **no new state law** compels cities to act, and local policies must still comply with remaining legal constraints (e.g. a 9th Circuit ruling *prior* to Grants Pass, which Arizona cities often heeded until mid-2024). - **Local Ordinances:** Some Arizona cities tightened existing anti-homeless rules. **Phoenix** in 2024 approved a plan to enforce a new **urban camping ban** citywide once alternate outdoor sanctioned camps could be established, including $100 fines for violations【1†L117-L125】【1†L119-L127】. **Tempe** and **Scottsdale** increased enforcement against park camping and vehicle dwelling. **Tucson** stepped up camp clearances near the Santa Cruz riverbed in late 2023.

These efforts often involved city police and were controversial given Arizona’s severe affordable housing shortage. ## Arkansas - **Local Crackdowns:** While Arkansas did not pass a known statewide anti-camping law in 2022–2025, there have been significant local enforcement efforts.

In Little Rock, police regularly conduct **encampment sweeps** along the Arkansas River and in downtown areas, enforcing city ordinances against camping or loitering.

In 2023, Little Rock officials also closed a large camp under I-30 after nearby residents complained, offering some shelter beds but leaving many unsheltered. **Fort Smith** and **Fayetteville** similarly enforce bans on camping in parks.

These actions are typically led by city police or parks departments. - **State Rhetoric and Proposed Laws:** Governor **Sarah Huckabee Sanders** (R) has emphasized “law and order” responses to homelessness.

In her 2023 State of the State, she cited vagrancy and squatting as issues to address【16†L28-L32】.

No such bill became law as of 2025, but the *climate* in Arkansas has been to empower police to remove “nuisance” encampments.

Notably, in 2023 Little Rock’s city board briefly considered an ordinance to ban panhandling at intersections (framed as a traffic safety issue), though it was tabled after legal warnings. ## California - **Wave of City Ordinances:** California, despite its generally liberal politics, has seen **numerous city-level anti-homeless ordinances** since 2022.

According to the National Homelessness Law Center, fully one-third of all new anti-camping measures passed nationwide in late 2023 were in California【1†L103-L110】【1†L105-L113】.

For example, **San Diego** (June 2023) outlawed camping on public property citywide if shelter is available, and even when shelters are full it banned encampments near schools, parks and transit hubs【5†L105-L113】. **Los Angeles** massively expanded enforcement of its anti-camping law (LAMC 41.18) in 2022–2023, creating over 100 “no homeless dwelling” buffer zones around schools and daycare centers.

Many ordinances were **amended post-Grants Pass** to remove prior requirements that enforcement pause if no shelter beds exist【1†L146-L154】.

Cities like **Oceanside, Paso Robles, and Hemet** explicitly deleted the “available shelter” clause in late 2024 so police can enforce bans at all times【1†L146-L154】.

Enforcement is typically by municipal police or code officers; some cities (e.g. **Los Angeles, San Francisco**) have dedicated “encampment resolution” teams that include police, sanitation, and outreach workers. - **State-Level Actions:** California’s **state government** has not passed laws criminalizing homelessness (in fact, state law *requires* cities to not criminalize sleeping if no shelter is available, per a 2021 statute codifying parts of *Martin v.

In August 2023, Newsom condemned a federal judge’s injunction stopping San Francisco from camping enforcement as “preposterous” and “inhumane,” arguing that encampments cause disorder and must be cleared【5†L195-L203】.

The state also launched the **CARE Court program (2022)**, allowing courts to mandate treatment plans for certain unhoused individuals with severe mental illness, which some advocates see as coercive.

Additionally, California has poured funding into housing and services ($15+ billion since 2021), but the slow pace of housing creation has led even liberal officials to endorse more enforcement.

The **League of California Cities and CA State Association of Counties** filed an amicus brief urging the Supreme Court to allow encampment bans, calling enforcement “a critical component” alongside services【5†L181-L189】【5†L191-L199】. ## Colorado - **Local Camping Bans:** Colorado has not passed any recent state law criminalizing homelessness, but cities maintain strict local policies. **Denver** has enforced its urban camping ban (in place since 2012) with renewed vigor post-pandemic.

In late 2023, Denver Mayor Mike Johnston briefly suspended sweeps during an emergency housing plan, but by 2024 the sweeps resumed, with police and public works regularly clearing downtown camps. **Colorado Springs** enforces a ban on camping along waterways (100-foot buffer from creeks) – an ordinance from 2018 cited by proponents as reducing unsheltered numbers【30†L247-L255】.

Boulder, for example, issued hundreds of tickets in 2022–2023 under its camping and tent bans, even as advocacy groups challenged these in court. - **Legislative Proposals:** Progressive legislators in Colorado have repeatedly introduced a **“Homeless Bill of Rights”** to protect activities like sleeping, eating, or sheltering oneself in public, but these bills did not pass (the last attempt stalled in 2022).

Instead, Colorado’s state efforts focused on funding housing and shelter (e.g. a 2023 law appropriated $200M for affordable housing and homeless services).

Despite that, the **strain on shelters** remains high, and local law enforcement still largely handles visible homelessness through trespass and public space ordinances. - **Notable Enforcement:** In October 2023, the city of **Aurora** (a Denver suburb) passed an ordinance banning unhoused people from camping within 50 feet of waterways and critical infrastructure, citing environmental and safety risks.

Aurora police in 2024 also started a controversial practice of **transporting homeless individuals** to a remote “designated camping area” outside the city limits – essentially attempting to relocate them out of sight.

This raised legal questions and pushback from advocates.

Colorado’s attorney general has warned municipalities to ensure any enforcement complies with constitutional rights (especially after *Grants Pass* removed the federal 8th Amendment issue, attention has turned to state constitutional protections in Colorado). ## Connecticut - **No State Anti-Homeless Laws:** Connecticut has not passed any laws in the past three years targeting unhoused people.

The state’s approach has remained largely focused on housing programs and shelter.

In fact, **Connecticut is one of the few states that effectively ended veteran homelessness and has low unsheltered numbers**.

The state did not entertain “camping ban” bills like those in the South/Midwest. - **Local Policies:** The major cities – **Hartford, New Haven, Bridgeport** – generally do *not* criminalize homelessness overtly.

Hartford cleared a small park encampment in 2022 for safety reasons), but these were handled through social services as much as policing. **New Haven** police have occasionally ticketed people for sleeping on the Green (a downtown park), but the city also opened more low-barrier shelters. - **Protective Measures:** Connecticut does have a **Homeless Person’s Bill of Rights** (enacted 2013) which affirms rights to move freely in public spaces and receive equal treatment by government.

In recent years, CT’s efforts have been to increase **housing vouchers and outreach**.

Thus, *no significant increases* in anti-homeless enforcement were noted from 2022–2025 beyond routine city park rules. ## Delaware - **Encampment Responses:** Delaware saw a few high-profile homeless encampments and responses in Wilmington and Newark but no new punitive laws.

The city worked with the Delaware DOT to post “no camping” notices on state-owned land and had police move people out, offering some temporary motel rooms. **New Castle County** in 2023 also cleared an encampment near Rt. 273 after multiple fires, citing safety.

These actions were ad hoc and not based on new ordinances – they relied on general trespassing laws. - **State Policy:** Delaware did not consider any anti-camping legislation.

On the contrary, state agencies focused on expanding shelter capacity (e.g. the Hope Center in New Castle County).

In 2023, some lawmakers proposed studying a **Homeless Bill of Rights** similar to Rhode Island’s, but it had not passed by 2025.

The Delaware State Housing Authority and local Continuum of Care agencies have generally opposed criminalizing homelessness, emphasizing housing placement instead.

Thus, Delaware’s **law enforcement involvement** has been limited to responding to specific issues (like clearing camps under highways for safety) rather than systematic criminalization. ## Florida - **Statewide Anti-Camping Law (2024):** In March 2024, Governor **Ron DeSantis** signed a new **anti-camping law** that *essentially bans outdoor encampments statewide*.

This law (sometimes referred to as Florida’s “Public Safety in Public Spaces Act”) requires that **cities and counties not allow camping on public property unless the site is officially designated** by the state Department of Children and Families (DCF)【15†L187-L195】【15†L199-L208】.

The law empowers DCF to set standards for any sanctioned homeless camps and mandates that local governments *prove* a lack of shelter space before setting up a sanctioned camp【15†L199-L208】.

It also **allows the state Attorney General, as well as private residents or businesses, to sue local governments** that “tolerate” encampments on public land starting in 2025【15†L211-L218】.

If such a lawsuit succeeds, the city/county can be forced to pay attorney fees and court costs【15†L211-L218】.

This remarkable provision essentially pressures local officials to strictly enforce anti-camping rules or face legal liability. - **Campsite Guidelines:** Under the 2024 law, counties *may* create designated camps for unhoused people on government property, but these sites must meet certain requirements (access to running water, bathrooms, and services like mental health and addiction treatment) **unless** the county is “fiscally constrained” and can’t afford it【15†L203-L210】.

These strict conditions make it very difficult to establish legal encampments; advocates worry it will lead to *forced dispersion* of homeless people with nowhere to go. - **Local Enforcement and Ordinances:** Even before the new law, Florida cities had ramped up enforcement. **Orlando** and **Orange County** in 2022 began a series of camp clearings, often just ahead of major events. **Miami** has long prohibited sidewalk camping and in 2022 it banned overnight encampments on downtown streets, with police conducting regular sweeps (sometimes controversially relocating people to a barrier island shelter). **Tampa** and **St.

Petersburg** enforce ordinances against sleeping in parks or right-of-ways; Tampa police issued hundreds of citations in 2022 for “lodging out-of-doors.” Several cities (e.g. **Clearwater, Ocala**) also enacted or revived **panhandling bans** especially targeting roadway medians and intersections – though these have been challenged in court (e.g. a federal suit against Jacksonville’s median panhandling ban is pending【34†L41-L49】【34†L53-L58】).

The new state law in 2024 adds further pressure on these cities to demonstrate compliance.

Florida’s approach thus combines **state mandates** with local ordinances, backed by law enforcement and in some cases the threat of litigation by the state Attorney General to ensure cities are cracking down. ## Georgia - **“Unsafe Camping” State Law (2023):** In 2023, Georgia enacted **SB 62**, a law that significantly shifts state policy on homelessness.

It also steers the state away from the federal “Housing First” approach: the law **directs state funding toward transitional housing programs that mandate sobriety or treatment**, rather than permanent housing【5†L99-L105】.

Specifically, it encourages the creation of *designated camping areas* (sanctioned encampments) and calls for a **state audit of all homelessness spending** to evaluate its effectiveness【5†L99-L107】.

This audit reflects a suspicion (fueled by the Cicero Institute model) that service providers and housing programs are “failing” and need more oversight. - **Enforcement Mechanisms:** Georgia’s law stops short of criminalizing homeless individuals at the state level, but it effectively criminalizes *local governments’ inaction*.

If a city does not enforce its anti-camping ordinances, it could be found in violation of state policy.

Advocates like the Southern Poverty Law Center note that the law passed **with no study of its impact on unhoused people**, and warn it will lead to more punitive sweeps【7†L183-L192】【7†L185-L193】.

Indeed, **Georgia was the second state (after Texas) to adopt Cicero Institute-inspired legislation**, aiming to make living on the street so untenable that people enter shelters or leave. - **Local Impact:** Following SB 62’s enactment (signed by Gov.

For example, **Savannah** and **Macon** stepped up camp clearouts in late 2023, coordinating with state officials.

In **Atlanta**, which already outlawed urban camping, Mayor Andre Dickens increased funding for enforcement teams, though also opened a new bridge shelter.

The SPLC’s 2024 report *Sheltering Injustice* documented how **Black communities in Georgia are disproportionately affected** by these criminalization measures【7†L189-L198】【7†L199-L207】.

The report and advocates like **Gina “Gigi” Azido Thompson (SPLC)** have been pressing to repeal SB 62 or mitigate its harms, but as of 2025 the law stands, with Georgia being a notable example of the punitive trend. ## Hawaii - **Sit-Lie and Park Bans:** Hawaii, particularly the City and County of **Honolulu (Oʻahu)**, has enforced strict anti-homeless ordinances for years.

Police regularly conduct **“Compassionate Disruption” sweeps** of homeless encampments in Honolulu’s parks and sidewalks, seizing property and forcing unhoused people to move.

Similar ordinances exist on **Maui** (no camping in county parks or beaches overnight) and on the **Big Island** (Hawaiʻi County bans sleeping in public parks without a permit).

The enforcement of these continued and even intensified post-pandemic as tourism rebounded and complaints increased. - **No New Statewide Law:** The Hawaii State Legislature did not pass any new anti-homeless laws in 2022–2025.

If anything, state efforts leaned toward assistance (for example, funding for the ʻOhana Zones pilot and outreach services).

However, **Governor Josh Green** and Honolulu Mayor Rick Blangiardi have supported continued enforcement of existing bans, arguing public spaces like Waikiki must be kept clear.

In 2023, Mayor Blangiardi also proposed **barring tents on sidewalks** across Honolulu, but that measure stalled due to legal concerns (given federal court precedents at the time).

After the 2024 SCOTUS Grants Pass ruling, Honolulu hinted it might revisit broader camping prohibitions since the 9th Circuit’s limitations no longer apply – a development to watch. - **Encampment Sweeps and Housing Crisis:** Hawaii’s homeless crisis is acute (especially unsheltered individuals in Honolulu and leeward Oʻahu).

These are conducted by Honolulu police with city maintenance crews, often resulting in discarded belongings.

Without sufficient affordable housing (Hawaii has one of the largest gaps), these enforcement actions have not reduced homelessness, only moved it out of tourist centers temporarily. ## Idaho - **Boise Legal Legacy:** Idaho was at the center of the *Martin v.

Boise* case (9th Cir. 2018) which had prohibited punishing homeless people for sleeping outside when no shelter is available.

While that was federal case law, Idaho itself did not pass any law countering it.

In fact, Idaho’s major cities largely complied with that precedent until 2024.

After the Supreme Court’s *Grants Pass* decision (mid-2024) removed the federal constraint, **Boise** indicated plans to resume stricter enforcement of its camping bans.

Boise has had an anti-camping ordinance on the books, but it was under a settlement from *Martin* that they wouldn’t enforce it in certain conditions.

City officials in late 2024 said they might re-evaluate that settlement given the legal change. - **State Legislation:** Idaho’s state legislature did not consider any anti-homeless bills in 2022–25. (Idaho is largely rural with lower visible homelessness, concentrated in Boise and Coeur d’Alene.) There was no “camping ban” bill like in neighboring states.

However, **Governor Brad Little** and some lawmakers focused on homelessness around the Idaho Falls area, directing state police in 2023 to assist local authorities in clearing transient camps on state lands near the Snake River.

This was done administratively rather than via new law. - **Local Enforcement:** Outside Boise, other cities took steps: **Coeur d’Alene** in 2023 began enforcing park closing hours and anti-camping rules more strictly after an uptick in unhoused individuals downtown, with support from county deputies. **Pocatello** and **Idaho Falls** also have ordinances against sleeping in public spaces that police enforce as needed (often by issuing warnings and trespass notices).

Idaho law (Idaho Code §18-705) already makes it a misdemeanor to refuse to leave public property when ordered by authorities, which is sometimes used to remove encampments. ## Illinois - **City Ordinance Surge:** Illinois itself (a Democratic-led state) did not enact anti-homeless state laws, but an *interesting trend* emerged in 2023–24: a **proliferation of municipal camping bans** across Illinois cities.

The **Illinois Municipal League (IML)** – an association of city governments – circulated a model ordinance to ban unauthorized camping, especially after the Grants Pass ruling.

Typically the first offense is a fine (around \$75 in many Illinois cities) and subsequent offenses could bring higher fines or even misdemeanor charges【1†L113-L119】.

Notably, **Illinois has a relatively smaller unsheltered population**, and many of these towns have few shelter beds – so advocates argue these ordinances simply push homeless individuals out or into jails. - **Enforcement & Penalties:** The Illinois city ordinances generally start with modest penalties (e.g. $75 fine in many places, $50 in others)【1†L113-L121】.

The enforcement is by local police departments.

For example, **Springfield, IL** (the state capital) passed a camping ban in 2024 and its police soon began issuing citations to people camping by the riverfront, offering shelter referrals but also clearing the camps regardless of shelter availability.

These moves in a blue state surprised some observers, but local officials claimed public pressure from residents and businesses “fed up” with encampments led to action. - **State Policies:** Illinois actually has a **Homeless Bill of Rights (2013)**, one of only a few states to have such a law.

It guarantees equal rights to use public space.

Homeless advocates have pointed out that the new local bans likely violate the spirit (if not letter) of this law.

However, enforcement has continued.

Meanwhile, Illinois state government under Gov.

Pritzker pursued a *different* approach: in 2022, the state launched a comprehensive initiative to reduce homelessness by 2025, focusing on housing and services.

No state resources were dedicated to criminalization.

Thus, the anti-homeless push in Illinois is mostly at the municipal level, not from Springfield. ## Indiana - **Proposed Statewide Camping Ban (2025):** In the 2025 legislative session, Indiana lawmakers considered **House Bill 1662**, which would criminalize “street camping” on public property statewide.

Authored by Rep. *Michelle Davis* (R), HB 1662 would make camping, sleeping or storing belongings on *any* public land (state or local) a **Class C misdemeanor**, punishable by up to 60 days in jail or a \$500 fine【9†L54-L62】【11†L269-L277】.

The bill included a provision that law enforcement must give a one-time warning and offer transportation to a shelter; if the person doesn’t relocate within 24 hours, they can be arrested【11†L269-L277】【11†L272-L280】.

An initial draft also sought to **ban use of state funds for permanent housing** for homelessness, but that clause faced pushback and was set to be removed by amendment【9†L62-L69】【9†L77-L83】. - **Cicero Institute Influence:** Rep.

Indeed, HB 1662 closely mirrored the model used in Texas, Missouri, etc., including a section that **prevents local governments from discouraging enforcement** or creating softer policies – with the Indiana Attorney General empowered to sue cities that don’t comply【11†L277-L285】【11†L279-L287】.

Homeless service providers (like Lafayette’s LTHC Housing Services) testified that criminalizing homelessness would “waste officers’ time…community resources…and add another barrier” to getting housing【9†L83-L91】【9†L87-L94】.

It appeared stalled, though language from it could resurface in other bills before the session ends【9†L69-L77】. *If* passed, it would make Indiana one of the few Midwestern states to adopt a statewide anti-camping law.

Meanwhile, Indiana cities like **Indianapolis** enforce their own measures – Indy had a policy requiring notice and outreach before clearing camps, but in 2023 Mayor Joe Hogsett began allowing more frequent clear-outs of downtown encampments after business complaints.

In 2023, **House File 565** was proposed to prohibit camping on public lands and redirect funding away from permanent housing solutions.

A renewed push in 2024 got through one chamber committee but stalled due to budget focus in the short session【13†L91-L99】. - **Local Responses:** Even without a state law, **local governments in Iowa have taken a tougher line**.

In 2023, the Des Moines City Council passed an ordinance making it illegal to block sidewalks or store personal property on public property, which has been used to break up tent camps.

Des Moines police conducted multiple sweeps along the Des Moines River in 2022–2024.

Similarly, **Cedar Rapids** and **Davenport** saw increased enforcement of park rules to disallow overnight stays. - **High-Profile Incident:** In late 2022, Des Moines drew attention for clearing out a large encampment under a freeway overpass just as winter set in.

Advocates sued to stop it, but a judge declined to intervene, noting the city had offered shelter beds.

This incident was cited by legislators as justification for a uniform state policy, arguing that allowing camps is unsafe.

Iowa’s homeless population is relatively small and mostly sheltered; however, unsheltered homelessness did tick up in recent years in urban centers, feeding these policy debates. ## Kansas - **Proposed State Ban (2023):** Kansas considered a statewide anti-camping bill in 2023, which was closely modeled on the Missouri and Texas laws.

The bill (unofficially dubbed the “Safe Streets Act”) was introduced by Republican lawmakers and would have made **camping on state-owned lands a misdemeanor** and **withheld state funds** from any city that didn’t enforce anti-camping measures.

It also included provisions to redirect homelessness program funding to short-term shelters.

In committee hearings, church groups and the Kansas Housing Resource Corporation testified against it, pointing out Kansas’s severe shortage of shelter beds.

For example, **Wichita** in 2022 strengthened enforcement of its existing ban on camping in public parks, citing safety and fire hazards after a blaze in a riverbank camp. **Overland Park** in 2023 passed an ordinance against solicitation at major intersections (targeting panhandling) – violators can be fined \$100.

And **Kansas City, KS (Wyandotte County)** collaborated with Kansas DOT to periodically clear camps under highway bridges, though without any new ordinance.

State agencies like KDOT and Kansas Highway Patrol have assisted in removing people living under overpasses on state property, especially after a tragic incident in 2022 when an unhoused individual was struck by a vehicle near a camp. ## Kentucky - **Criminalization Law in 2024:** In April 2024, Kentucky’s GOP-led legislature overrode Governor Andy Beshear’s veto to enact **a broad criminal justice bill (HB 353)** that included an anti-camping provision.

This new law, effective July 2024, **makes camping or sleeping in public places (such as sidewalks, parks, under bridges) a Class B misdemeanor**【15†L147-L151】.

On first offense, police must give a warning and an offer of services; if the person refuses or is found violating again, they can be charged.

A Class B misdemeanor in Kentucky can mean up to 90 days in jail, though the law here specifies that typically a fine (around \$250) or short jail stay would apply for repeat offenses. - **“Self-Defense” Against Homeless Campers:** Uniquely, Kentucky’s law contains a controversial clause allowing **property owners to use physical force to defend their property from unlawful camping**【15†L173-L180】.

Specifically, if a person experiencing homelessness sets up camp on private property and refuses to leave after being told, the property owner is justified in using force if the camper threatens force or is committing criminal trespass【15†L173-L181】.

Beshear (D) vetoed the bill, stating it would “criminalize homelessness and significantly increase incarceration costs without any additional appropriation”【15†L163-L171】.

The law also instructs local governments to designate possible **“safe camping” sites** away from public use areas (with water and toilets ideally)【15†L169-L177】, though no funding was provided for this. - **Enforcement and Aftermath:** After going into effect, cities like **Louisville and Lexington** expressed concern.

Smaller cities, however, saw some enforcement: **Bowling Green** police in late 2024 cited multiple people under the new law for camping behind shopping centers.

This law’s enactment despite the governor’s veto highlights the punitive swing in Kentucky’s approach, whereas previously the focus was more on drug treatment and shelters. ## Louisiana - **Local Ordinances and Sweeps:** Louisiana did not pass statewide anti-homeless legislation between 2022 and 2025, but several cities ramped up enforcement. **New Orleans** periodically carries out large encampment sweeps, especially in the downtown and under I-10.

The city has ordinances against obstruction of sidewalks and public camping that it enforces intermittently. **Baton Rouge** in 2023 increased policing of panhandling at intersections after local law changes – police began issuing $200 fines under a new ordinance prohibiting standing on medians (similar to those seen elsewhere for “pedestrian safety”). **Shreveport** also passed an ordinance in 2022 prohibiting sleeping in downtown parks overnight. - **State Attitude:** In the Legislature, a few bills were floated.

One in 2023 would have made it a misdemeanor to repeatedly refuse shelter and remain on public property when offered (sometimes dubbed a “services refusal” law), but it didn’t advance out of committee.

Louisiana’s focus at the state level has been more on establishing low-barrier shelters and clearing homeless camps near highways through the DOTD.

Notably, **State Police** cooperated with local officials in 2024 to remove people living along interstate underpasses in Lake Charles and Lafayette, citing health and safety after reports of fires. - **“Banishment” Controversy:** In 2022, New Orleans’ approach drew criticism when media reported that some police officers were effectively **banishing homeless individuals** – transporting them out of the tourist areas and even across parish lines.

This was not formal policy, and the NOPD claimed it was only offering rides to shelters, but advocates suspect otherwise.

Overall, Louisiana has addressed homelessness in a piecemeal punitive way locally (to “clean up” visible areas), without a coherent state policy beyond standard trespass and loitering laws. ## Maine - **Median Ban in Bangor:** Maine generally emphasizes support over criminalization, but one notable action was **Bangor’s ordinance (June 2023)** banning people from standing on or loitering in street medians less than 6 feet wide on high-speed roads【34†L113-L121】【34†L115-L123】.

Bangor’s law was passed after complaints about panhandling at busy intersections; violators can be cited (a fine or misdemeanor for repeated offense). - **Encampments and State Response:** In late 2022, a large encampment near downtown **Portland, ME** became a political flashpoint.

MaineHousing (the state housing authority) and Governor Janet Mills’ administration supported providing emergency shelter to those displaced but did not intervene legally. **Augusta**, the state capital, also cleared an encampment by the Kennebec River in summer 2023, with police enforcing trespass on state land. - **State Policy Stance:** Maine has not criminalized homelessness at the state level.

If anything, in 2022 Maine’s Legislature considered a Homeless Persons’ Bill of Rights (modeled on Rhode Island’s) – but it did not pass.

Governor Mills has focused on increasing shelter capacity, especially during winters.

That said, the **visibility of homelessness in Portland and Bangor** (exacerbated by a surge of asylum seekers in shelters) has led to some public pressure for stricter measures.

Portland in 2024 debated a new loitering ordinance around certain public buildings but instead opted for more funding to outreach and mental health responders. **In sum, Maine’s main “criminalizing” step was the Bangor median law**, with most other actions being individual camp clearances using existing laws. ## Maryland - **No State Criminalization Law:** Maryland did not pass any anti-homeless legislation recently.

The state government, under Republican Gov.

Maryland’s approach remained oriented toward services, not law enforcement. - **Baltimore Encampments:** The City of **Baltimore** has had some contentious encampment clearings.

In early 2023, Baltimore City officials cleared a prominent encampment in the Jonestown area, offering residents placement in shelter or motel rooms.

Baltimore Police also enforce against panhandling *aggressively* in certain areas (e.g. around the Inner Harbor), often by citing other violations like impeding traffic. - **Smaller Jurisdictions:** In 2022, **Hagerstown** made news for its handling of an encampment on railroad property downtown.

The city attempted to evict the camp; advocates went to court, but a judge allowed the eviction to proceed as the city opened a temporary sanctioned camping site elsewhere.

That site was short-lived and closed in 2023, leaving many without shelter. **Montgomery County** (suburban DC) generally avoids criminalizing – it provides storage for encampment residents’ belongings during cleanups and connects people to housing programs.

Overall, Maryland’s efforts have been more about **balance and relocation** rather than new penalties, with the exception of police in some towns using existing laws (e.g. trespass, park closure rules at night). ## Massachusetts - **Right to Shelter (Boston):** Massachusetts is known for a *right-to-shelter* statewide policy (unique aside from New York) which requires providing shelter to homeless families.

While this has strained capacity due to an influx of migrants in 2023, it means the state cannot simply push people to the streets.

There have been **no new anti-encampment laws** at the state level; instead, the focus is on expanding shelters/hotels for those in need. - **Boston’s “Mass and Cass” Crackdowns:** The major issue has been in Boston around the Massachusetts Ave & Melnea Cass Blvd area (“Mass and Cass”), which has seen a large open-air drug use and homeless encampment.

In October 2023, Mayor Michelle Wu ordered a sweep of Mass and Cass, enforcing a policy that tents are not allowed on public sidewalks.

Police, accompanied by outreach workers, removed tents and structures and directed people to shelter or treatment.

The city also instituted a section of **“no tents” declaration** in that zone via a public health ordinance.

In general, Massachusetts leans away from criminalization, but the urgency around visible drug use and tents at Mass and Cass has led to repeated **police-enforced disbanding of encampments** there, even under threat of federal injunction (a lawsuit by the ACLU was filed in late 2023 challenging Boston’s tactics, on grounds of property rights and disability rights). ## Michigan - **Homeless Bill of Rights Proposal:** In Michigan, rather than anti-homeless laws, a *protective* measure was introduced.

State Rep. **Emily Dievendorf** (D) in 2023 put forth a “Homeless Bill of Rights” bill to ban discrimination based on housing status and ensure the right to rest in public spaces (among other rights)【5†L79-L87】【5†L91-L99】.

Its lack of progress suggests other priorities took precedence. - **Local Enforcement:** Michigan’s major cities have generally not adopted new criminalization ordinances lately. **Detroit** has focused on shelter and housing programs, though Detroit police do occasionally clear encampments in certain areas (e.g., near Hart Plaza or under freeway bridges) if they pose health hazards.

In 2022, the city cleared an encampment on city-owned land slated for development, offering shelter placements to those displaced. **Grand Rapids** has a strict parks rule and in 2023 stepped up removal of camps along the Grand River after floods and safety issues.

Police there issue warnings and then citations for trespass if needed. - **Notable Incident:** Ann Arbor, a relatively progressive city, had a controversy in 2023 when the city closed a popular downtown library during overnight hours specifically to deter unhoused individuals from sheltering there.

Michigan’s state agencies continue to emphasize housing-first; in fact, Michigan was one of the states whose cities (Detroit, etc.) *declined* to join an amicus brief asking the Supreme Court to allow more encampment enforcement, signaling a more sympathetic stance compared to many states. ## Minnesota - **Encampment Clearing in Minneapolis/St.

Paul:** Minnesota saw no new state law criminalizing homelessness.

These operations, carried out by Minneapolis Police and city public works, led to the **destruction of tents and personal property**, drawing outrage from activists.

There is no ordinance explicitly banning camping citywide, but Minneapolis uses general trespass law and park rules.

Paul similarly cleared its largest camp in Fall 2022 (at Kellogg Boulevard), coordinating with non-profits to offer shelter. - **State & Local Response:** In response to these issues, Governor **Tim Walz** approved emergency funding in 2023 to help expand shelter and outreach in the Twin Cities, rather than endorsing any criminal penalties.

Yet enforcement continues: by late 2024, Minneapolis had closed at least 15 encampments that year.

The **Minnesota ACLU** has been monitoring and considering legal action for potential constitutional violations (especially property seizure and lack of notice). - **Greater Minnesota:** Outside the metro, homelessness is less visible.

Overall, Minnesota maintains a housing-friendly posture at the state level (investing in affordable housing, etc.), but on the ground in cities, **police-enforced encampment disbandments** have been the primary tool to address unsheltered homelessness. ## Mississippi - **Local Policing:** Mississippi did not enact statewide policies against homelessness in 2022–2025.

Homelessness in Mississippi is relatively low in numbers and mostly concentrated in **Jackson** and Gulf Coast towns.

In **Jackson**, police occasionally enforce city ordinances against vagrancy, especially in business districts.

The city has extremely limited shelter space, so enforcement is sporadic. - **Coast Cities:** In **Biloxi** and **Gulfport**, local authorities have been known to arrest individuals for **panhandling or “aggressive solicitation”** under existing local laws.

Gulfport in 2022 ran an operation to deter panhandlers by offering work-for-food programs and warning others they could be fined for begging. **Hattiesburg** enforced park curfews to keep people from camping overnight in public parks. - **State Attitude:** Mississippi’s state government has not spent much time on homelessness policy at all, neither criminalizing nor substantially funding solutions.

In 2021 there was some controversy when a state lawmaker proposed a ban on “urban camping,” but it didn’t progress.

The focus in Mississippi at state level has been more on addressing mental health system gaps (which indirectly affect homelessness) due to a federal case about mental health services.

Thus, Mississippi saw *no notable trend up or down* – just continued low-level enforcement of existing ordinances and reliance on charity groups for relief. ## Missouri - **Statewide Camping Ban (2022, Struck Down 2023):** Missouri was one of the first states to pass a sweeping anti-homeless law, in 2022.

The law (originally an amendment to HB 1606) **made sleeping or camping on state-owned land a Class C misdemeanor**, punishable by a $750 fine or 15 days jail for repeat offenses【24†L133-L141】【24†L135-L143】.

It also allowed the **Missouri Attorney General to sue cities** that fail to enforce the ban【24†L58-L66】【24†L59-L67】, and threatened to **withhold state funding for homelessness services from local governments that didn’t comply** (unless their homeless rate dropped below the state average)【24†L93-L100】【24†L94-L102】.

Additionally, it **redirected all state homeless funds away from permanent housing to temporary “designated camping sites” with services**【24†L99-L107】【24†L147-L155】.

This law took effect January 2023. - **Implementation and Legal Challenge:** Immediately, there was confusion and pushback.

In practice, some police in Missouri began warning people in camps on MoDOT (state highway) property that they must leave or face penalties.

However, in December 2023 the Missouri Supreme Court **struck down the 2022 law on a technicality**, ruling it violated the state constitution’s single-subject rule (the bill’s provisions were not clearly related to the bill title)【5†L97-L105】【13†L79-L87】.

This was a relief to service providers who had feared funding cuts. - **Post-Strike Aftermath:** Despite the court decision, the mindset has shifted in Missouri.

In the interim, some cities carried on with stricter enforcement anyway.

For example, **Springfield, MO** ramped up enforcement of park camping bans, citing the (now-defunct) state law as justification. **Columbia, MO** in 2023 passed its own ordinance aligning with the state’s intent, banning camping on city property and empowering police to clear camps with 24-hour notice.

The **Missouri Department of Mental Health** had begun reallocating grants toward short-term shelter projects due to the 2022 law; some of those initiatives continued (like “safe camping” sites in St.

In summary, Missouri pioneered the aggressive approach, briefly had it on the books, but saw it overturned – yet many local policies still reflect that approach. ## Montana - **Urban Camping Ordinances:** Montana’s few urban centers dealt with some homelessness by enforcement of existing rules. **Missoula**, for instance, has a law against camping or structures in parks and along riverbanks; city police periodically clear camps along the Clark Fork River, especially after complaints.

In 2023 Missoula County also passed an ordinance prohibiting unauthorized camping on county-owned lands (like fairgrounds or conservation areas), with violators subject to trespass citations. **Billings** experienced an increase in visible homelessness downtown; in 2022 Billings police started more strictly enforcing against sleeping in storefront alcoves and set up a unit to patrol for vagrancy overnight.

No new law was needed – they used existing loitering statutes. - **State Legislation:** Montana did not propose any anti-homeless bills recently.

In fact, in 2023 the legislature (GOP-controlled) was more focused on other issues; homelessness wasn’t a top agenda item.

The state did secure some federal funding to expand shelter capacity in places like Bozeman and Kalispell, indicating more of a services approach. - **Notable Local Policy:** In late 2021 (just before our timeframe), **Bozeman** passed a no-camping ordinance for city parks and parking garages after some incidents downtown.

That continued to be enforced in 2022–2025. **Great Falls** has a long-standing ban on “camping” in city limits outside of designated campgrounds – enforced by police moving people along.

There is a strong reliance on charities (often faith-based) to coax individuals into shelters rather than formal criminalization, but those who refuse are often cited for trespass or public nuisance. ## Nebraska - **Omaha and Lincoln:** Nebraska did not see new state laws on this issue.

In 2022–2023, Omaha police conducted sweeps of a few camps along the Missouri River and under bridges near downtown.

Omaha also beefed up enforcement of an ordinance against obstructing sidewalks to prevent encampments downtown. **Lincoln** similarly has an ordinance barring camping on public property; in 2023 the city cleared a notable camp in the Salt Creek area, relocating about 20 people to shelter or out of the area. - **State Actions:** The Nebraska Legislature did not take up a camping-ban bill recently.

The **Nebraska Attorney General’s office** informally advised cities that they could enforce ordinances as long as they had offered shelter options. - **Panhandling:** Lincoln in 2022 updated an ordinance to prohibit aggressive panhandling (approaching cars, etc.), and police have issued citations under that.

Omaha’s downtown business district saw a joint effort with police in 2023 to keep transients from sleeping in building entryways at night – sometimes using trespass orders signed by property owners. - **Conclusion for NE:** In summary, Nebraska’s approach hasn’t dramatically changed – existing ordinances are used a bit more aggressively post-2024, but no new laws were made.

Advocates like **Matt Wallen of Together Omaha** have urged investment in housing rather than policing, but the state’s modest homelessness numbers keep it a lower-profile issue. ## Nevada - **Las Vegas Crackdown:** Nevada’s largest city, **Las Vegas**, has been at the forefront of anti-homeless laws.

Back in 2019, Las Vegas passed an ordinance making it illegal to camp or sleep on downtown streets if shelter beds are available.

In November 2023 – after the Grants Pass ruling – the Las Vegas City Council **expanded its ban citywide** to prohibit camping or “lodging” on any public property at any time, regardless of shelter availability【1†L59-L67】【1†L79-L87】.

Las Vegas City Marshals (who police city property) immediately began enforcing the broadened law – for example, in late 2024 they issued citations to several people sleeping near the Fremont Street area【1†L131-L139】.

One 55-year-old man cited twice was told a third offense could bring jail; indeed, some cited individuals have upcoming court dates where they face up to 6 months behind bars【1†L131-L139】. - **Reno and Others:** **Reno** has an ordinance against sitting or lying on downtown sidewalks and also bans camping along the Truckee River.

Reno stepped up enforcement in 2022–2023 by conducting regular sweeps and redirecting people to the large **CAC (Community Assistance Center) shelter**.

Reno police hand out frequent citations for unlawful camping (over 1,200 tickets in 2022). **Henderson** and **North Las Vegas** also passed ordinances in 2022 mirroring Las Vegas’s ban to ensure homeless from Las Vegas don’t simply migrate to their areas. - **State Policy:** The State of Nevada itself did not enact any anti-homeless law, but state government has supported local crackdowns indirectly.

Additionally, Nevada’s Attorney General has not challenged these city laws.

On the supportive side, in 2023 the state did allocate more money for rapid rehousing programs in Clark County.

But with homelessness rising (Nevada saw increases in unsheltered counts), **the dominant visible response has been law enforcement**.

Notably, in a series of articles in early 2025, advocates warned that since the Supreme Court green-lit these bans, Las Vegas and other Nevada cities were on track to **criminalize homelessness more than ever**【1†L78-L87】【1†L99-L107】 – a trend being watched as a potential “test case” for whether criminalization can survive legal and moral scrutiny. ## New Hampshire - **Manchester Ordinance Tightened (2024):** In July 2024, shortly after the Supreme Court ruling, **Manchester** – New Hampshire’s largest city – voted to **strengthen its ban on camping on city property**【23†L49-L57】【23†L51-L59】.

Critics noted this makes it easier for officials to evict people even when no alternative shelter exists. - **Legal Back-and-Forth:** Earlier in January 2023, Manchester had already attempted to evict a sidewalk encampment around a shelter.

Advocates (ACLU-NH and NH Legal Assistance) sued to stop it, citing *Martin v.

A state judge **ultimately allowed the eviction**, finding that at that moment, shelter space was available【23†L107-L115】【23†L145-L151】.

However, the judge also hinted that *if* no shelter were available, a forced eviction could violate New Hampshire’s own Constitution (which bans “cruel or unusual” punishment)【23†L133-L141】【23†L143-L151】.

The Grants Pass decision in 2024 removed federal protection, but the ACLU of NH has explicitly warned cities that the **state constitution may still prohibit criminalizing homelessness**【23†L73-L81】.

Officials there eventually cleared it with police, but provided storage and help.

The **state government** under Gov.

Chris Sununu hasn’t passed punitive laws; instead, it’s been increasing funding for shelter beds.

In summary, *Manchester stands out* for hardening its stance (likely to face a legal test), while the rest of NH is cautious due to potential state constitutional issues. ## New Jersey - **Local Enforcement in Cities:** New Jersey did not pass state laws targeting the homeless.

Major cities like **Newark**, **Jersey City**, **Atlantic City**, and **Camden** have addressed encampments through local actions. **Newark** in 2022 used a combination of police and sanitation workers to clear a tent cluster under Rt. 21, citing health violations; they offered placement in the city’s low-threshold shelter. **Atlantic City** periodically cracks down on panhandling and sleeping under the Boardwalk (using a 2019 ordinance).

In summer 2023, ahead of tourist season, Atlantic City police removed several homeless individuals camping under the piers and boardwalk, enforcing ordinances against public camping and obstruction. - **Right to Shelter Lawsuit:** One noteworthy development: In late 2023, a coalition of advocates filed a lawsuit seeking to compel New Jersey to provide emergency shelter to all unhoused people in cold weather, effectively pushing for a *right-to-shelter* in extreme conditions.

The litigation is ongoing, but it has put pressure on the state to focus on housing rather than enforcement. - **NJ Transit and Police:** New Jersey Transit (state agency) has in the past year stepped up removal of homeless individuals from train stations and terminals after complaints.

NJ Transit Police launched “Operation Gateway” in 2023 to connect homeless people at Newark Penn Station with services but also to bar them from staying in stations overnight.

This sometimes leads to citations for trespassing after hours. - **Overall:** The New Jersey statehouse has concentrated on funding affordable housing trust funds and motels for Code Blue (winter emergency) rather than punitive measures.

The ethos in NJ’s executive branch is to avoid litigation by not explicitly criminalizing homelessness.

Therefore, enforcement is mostly at the municipal level using pre-existing ordinances on loitering, panhandling (to the extent constitutional), and park rules. ## New Mexico - **Statewide Median Ban Proposal:** In 2023, Governor **Michelle Lujan Grisham** (D) pushed a proposal to ban standing on narrow road medians statewide, directly aimed at panhandlers on medians【34†L48-L57】【34†L81-L90】.
