By end of 2024, New Mexico hadn’t enacted it, partly due to concerns over free speech and efficacy. - **Local Ordinances:** **Santa Fe** and **Española** already have city laws barring standing on certain medians (Española’s 2022 law was a model for the governor’s idea)【34†L79-L87】【34†L107-L115】. **Albuquerque**, the largest city, does not ban panhandling outright (previous attempts struck down), but it has used other ordinances to curb tent camps.

In mid-2023, Albuquerque officials, facing growing encampments along the Bosque (river) and near downtown, began enforcing park closure rules and a fire ordinance to disperse camps, especially after some shootings at an encampment.

They also tried a unique approach: a city ordinance lets them issue a “notice to vacate” an encampment with 72-hour notice and store belongings for up to 30 days.

Some advocates argue the focus on medians is a distraction from the real issue (lack of housing and treatment).

Meanwhile, **Las Cruces** in southern NM has focused on expanding shelter, not enforcement.

New Mexico’s emphasis seems mixed – the **governor’s push for a median law** indicates some appetite for visible enforcement, but legislative resistance and local contexts have tempered broader criminalization. ## New York - **New York City’s Crackdowns:** While New York State has a right-to-shelter for homeless people (court-ordered in NYC since 1981), New York City under Mayor **Eric Adams** has launched aggressive measures affecting unhoused individuals.

Over spring 2022, more than 300 makeshift street camps in NYC were dismantled, with police throwing away tents and bedding.

Outreach teams offered shelter placements, but acceptance rates were low.

Adams also deployed a special **NYPD task force in the subways** to remove people who were sleeping in trains or stations; coupled with a policy to involuntarily hospitalize some mentally ill homeless persons encountered in public (announced Nov 2022).

These moves sparked controversy and civil rights concerns, but Adams argued they were about connecting people to care and keeping public spaces safe. - **Right-to-Shelter Under Strain:** In 2023, NYC’s shelter system was overwhelmed by new arrivals (including asylum seekers), leading Mayor Adams to *attempt to suspend parts of the right-to-shelter*.

In October 2023 he filed in court to relieve the city of the obligation to provide shelter to adult homeless men, citing resource emergency【6†L1-L4】.

Essentially, the city signaled it cannot house everyone and sought flexibility to potentially deny shelter – which homeless advocates say would lead to more street homelessness and possibly more punitive measures.

The outcome could reshape NYC’s approach and by extension influence other jurisdictions. - **Statewide Law Enforcement:** Outside NYC, some upstate cities took a harder line. **Syracuse** in 2022 evicted people from a long-standing camp known as “Camp Hope” on city land, invoking health codes. **Albany** in 2023 passed an ordinance against panhandling aggressively near ATM machines and parking meters (small fine for violators).

However, New York State as a whole did not criminalize homelessness; in fact, Gov.

Kathy Hochul’s administration put hundreds of millions into housing and even required upstate counties to find shelter for migrants/homeless being sent from NYC.

The tension between NYC’s legal shelter mandate and rising numbers has been *the* dominant issue, overshadowing attempts to criminalize behaviors.

But if NYC’s right-to-shelter gets curtailed, there is concern among advocates that it could usher in more West Coast-style encampments and possibly enforcement to remove them.

As of 2025 though, NYC and New York State remain more focused on providing shelter (however strained) than on laws punishing homeless people directly. ## North Carolina - **Municipal Actions:** North Carolina’s legislature did not pass anti-homeless laws in 2022–25, but cities made moves. **Charlotte** experienced a notable encampment called “Tent City” in early 2021 (just before our window), which was cleared by public health order due to a rat infestation.

In 2022–2023, Charlotte officials proactively broke up any re-forming camps, citing trespassing on county land. **Raleigh** in 2023 passed an ordinance prohibiting camping on city greenways after some camps were found along trails – police now post 24-hour notices to vacate and then remove tents. **Wilmington, NC** in 2024 adopted a ban on panhandling and other pedestrian presence on medians (similar to those discussed nationally)【34†L52-L60】. - **State-Level Attitude:** The Republican-majority General Assembly in NC focused on other issues; if anything, some lawmakers wanted to preempt local panhandling laws for uniformity, but it didn’t materialize.

One interesting twist: In 2021, NC had passed a law stiffening penalties for *aggressive* panhandling (making repeated harassment a misdemeanor), but that was already on the books by 2022.

Enforcement of that has been minimal, though, due to First Amendment considerations. - **Durham and Asheville:** **Durham** in 2022 had a confrontation where a group of unhoused people took refuge in a downtown plaza; the city eventually got police to remove them, but also then convened a Homelessness Task Force to find better solutions. **Asheville**, a city with a visible homeless population, increased police presence downtown in 2023 after business complaints.

So enforcement continues in an ad hoc way using general laws (trespass, public park rules). ## North Dakota - **Minimal Issues:** North Dakota has one of the smallest homeless populations and did not enact any laws on this topic.

The largest city, **Fargo**, reported an uptick in unsheltered individuals in 2022, which led the Fargo Police to enforce against camping along the Red River downtown – mostly by asking people to move and issuing trespass warnings for repeated refusal.

Fargo also banned overnight camping on city-owned parking lots in 2023 after some RV dwellers were using a lot near the civic center. - **Statewide Response:** The state government’s role has been negligible here.

In winter, North Dakota leverages shelters and makes sure people aren’t sleeping outside due to extreme cold, often by compassionate law enforcement (bringing individuals to shelters or hospitals).

In Bismarck and Minot, police occasionally respond to complaints of panhandling or vagrancy, but usually handle it informally. - **Law Enforcement Climate:** If anything, North Dakota police might charge someone with minor offenses like public intoxication or trespassing if they refuse to relocate from private property.

But there’s been no sign of ND adopting something like a camping ban law.

The state did quietly support an amicus brief by some cities to the Supreme Court (Grants Pass case) pushing for more local authority to remove encampments【5†L107-L115】, likely because they foresee potential issues in Fargo or Grand Forks.

So far, though, ND’s situation hasn’t demanded harsh measures. ## Ohio - **Local Ordinances:** Ohio’s state government hasn’t passed anti-homeless laws recently, but cities have acted. **Cincinnati** in 2022 reinstated enforcement of a seldom-used ordinance prohibiting encampments on public property after a large camp sprang up downtown.

The city gave a 2-week notice, then cleared the camp, citing the local law. **Columbus** has no camping ban ordinance, but Columbus Police have used park rules and other laws to disperse camps, especially near the Scioto riverfront and under highway bridges.

In 2023 Columbus city officials started working on a formal policy to address camps after clearing a big one near downtown (with social workers on hand). - **Cleveland** had an interesting turn: Rather than crack down, in 2022 the city actually worked with the ACLU to develop protocols for when and how encampments could be cleared, trying to ensure due notice and storage of belongings.

Nevertheless, Cleveland has enforced some removals, like one under a bridge before the MLB All-Star Game in 2022 – a move criticized as “cleaning up” for optics. - **Statehouse:** The Ohio General Assembly did not weigh in with any new law to criminalize homelessness.

Some individual lawmakers from Cleveland area, instead, have called for more affordable housing funds.

Ohio has focused state efforts on opioid crisis response which intersects with homelessness, but not on penalizing homelessness per se. - **Dayton and Others:** In **Dayton**, a federal judge in 2018 had issued an order protecting an encampment; by 2022 that camp was gone and Dayton avoided aggressive enforcement, instead creating additional shelter.

So Ohio sees *localized measures* but nothing sweeping or coordinated at the state level. ## Oklahoma - **Statewide Camping Misdemeanor (2024):** Oklahoma enacted a law in April 2024 that criminalizes camping on state-owned property.

The law, passed by the Republican legislature and signed by Gov.

Kevin Stitt, **makes it a misdemeanor (punishable by up to a \$50 fine or 15 days in jail) to camp on state public lands**【13†L99-L107】.

On first offense, police are instructed to offer to transport the person to a shelter or services, and only if they refuse assistance can a citation be issued【13†L99-L107】【13†L111-L119】.

This law closely followed the Cicero Institute model and was promoted as a way to push people toward help.

Critics note that with fines or even a short jail stint, it will likely complicate people’s ability to secure housing later【13†L37-L45】【13†L52-L59】. - **Local Concerns:** Service providers in Oklahoma City and Tulsa pointed out that **there are not enough shelter beds** to accommodate everyone the law might displace【15†L242-L250】【15†L261-L269】.

For instance, a 2022 count found OKC had about 200 more unsheltered people than available year-round shelter beds【15†L259-L268】.

The law doesn’t ensure additional shelter, raising concerns that people will just be driven into hiding.

Additionally, police in Oklahoma City indicated uncertainty on enforcement specifics by press time【13†L111-L119】.

They cannot cite someone unless they first offer help and the person refuses【13†L111-L119】. - **Oklahoma City Initiatives:** OKC has a program called “Key to Home” that pairs outreach workers with encampment residents to secure housing vouchers, then clears the camp after housing people【13†L127-L134】.

Nonetheless, with the new state law, **Oklahoma City and Tulsa police** did begin integrating its provisions by late 2024 – issuing warnings at camps on state land (like along highways) and preparing to cite repeat cases.

The law took effect November 2024; as of early 2025, data on its enforcement is not yet fully available. ## Oregon - **Post-Boise Legal Changes:** Oregon was ground-zero for *Johnson v.

Before SCOTUS intervened, Oregon had strict *judicially-imposed* limits: a federal district judge in 2020 ruled Grants Pass’s camping fines unconstitutional, and the 9th Circuit upheld that in 2022.

Thus, through 2022–early 2024, Oregon cities were operating under Martin/Boise rules (can’t enforce camping bans if no shelter).

However, in June 2024 the Supreme Court reversed that by ruling in Grants Pass’s favor【19†L109-L118】.

In May 2023, anticipating a legal shift, Portland’s City Council passed an ordinance banning camping on city streets *during daytime hours* (8am to 8pm) and limiting the size and location of any night camps.

Once the Supreme Court decision came, **Portland moved toward a near-total ban**: in late 2024, the Council approved phased plans to prohibit *all* street camping and establish a few large sanctioned camp sites instead.

Enforcement ramped up – by end of 2024, Portland police were dispersing camps that previously they had left alone due to the Boise ruling.

Other cities like **Salem** and **Medford** quickly removed prior policies that required available shelter before enforcement. - **Strict Enforcement Examples:** **Grants Pass**, now legally victorious, began enforcing its ordinances prohibiting sleeping in parks, on sidewalks, or in vehicles with fines and potential jail time【19†L109-L118】. **Eugene** and **Bend** also introduced tougher rules in late 2024 banning camping in certain zones (near schools, waterways, etc.) and empowering quicker camp cleanups. **Richland, WA** – sorry wrong state.

In Oregon, smaller cities in the Willamette Valley like **Corvallis** and **Albany** have debated new anti-camping laws as well. - **State Government:** Oregon’s state legislature in 2023 actually passed a law *protecting* homeless individuals: HB 3115, effective July 2023, requires local anti-camping ordinances to be “objectively reasonable” regarding time/place/manner, essentially codifying the Martin v.

Boise standard into state law.

This was intended to ensure some protection even if federal law changed.

Now that SCOTUS did change course, it’s unclear how HB 3115 will interplay – possibly through state court challenges.

Indeed, **advocates filed suits in Oregon state courts** (e.g., the ACLU supporting challenges in **Spokane, WA** under that state’s constitution, and likely to do similar in Oregon) arguing the state constitutions can forbid cruel punishment where the federal court won’t【19†L119-L127】【19†L177-L185】.

Oregon’s attorney general has not yet taken a stance on that.

Meanwhile, Governor Tina Kotek has focused on funding and declared homelessness emergencies in many counties, but not on criminalization.

The dichotomy is sharp: *localities forging ahead with bans* vs. *state policy leaning toward housing assistance with some baseline rights*. ## Pennsylvania - **Philadelphia’s Approach:** Philadelphia has generally avoided criminalizing homelessness in recent years, relying on outreach and services.

Philly has a legal encampment resolution protocol from a 2018 consent decree that emphasizes offering housing before clearing camps.

Police were present but the emphasis was on service navigation to treatment or shelter. - **Other Cities:** **Pittsburgh** rarely sees large encampments, but in 2022 it cleared a notable tent camp under a highway overpass near downtown after a stabbing incident.

They used a city ordinance against encumbering the right-of-way. **Harrisburg** in 2023 faced controversy for removing a small encampment from a riverfront park just before July 4 events, seen by some as driven by image concerns. - **State Legislature:** Pennsylvania did not pursue anti-homeless laws.

The new governor (Josh Shapiro) has not emphasized any punitive homeless policies. - **Law Enforcement:** In some smaller towns, anecdotal reports suggest police “ticket and move” homeless individuals using minor offenses (open container, public urination, etc.) rather than any homelessness-specific law.

E.g., in **Erie**, police in 2023 patrolled a known encampment and issued citations for littering to pressure folks to relocate.

These are localized tactics rather than formal policy. ## Rhode Island - **Homeless Bill of Rights & Violations:** Rhode Island is one of three states with a **Homeless Bill of Rights** (enacted 2012) that affirms the rights of homeless individuals to move freely in public and not face discrimination in housing, employment, or public services due to their housing status.

Despite this, advocates report that authorities often **ignore the law in practice** when clearing encampments【5†L147-L155】【5†L153-L160】.

In Providence, for example, police have conducted sweeps of camps (such as a prominent one in 2022 along I-95) without providing adequate notice or alternatives, arguably in violation of the state law – yet no enforcement mechanism exists for the homeless bill of rights, so it has “no teeth”【5†L147-L155】【5†L151-L158】. - **Providence & Supreme Court Appeal:** Providence in 2023 joined several cities in an amicus brief to the U.S.

Supreme Court urging it to overturn the Martin precedent to give cities more leeway to clear encampments【5†L107-L115】.

This is striking given RI’s protective law, indicating Providence officials’ frustration with encampments.

Indeed, Providence had a federal court order in late 2022 temporarily stopping it from clearing an encampment on state land; the city eventually got approval to remove it in 2023 after opening additional shelter beds. - **State and Municipal Actions:** Rhode Island’s state government under Gov.

Dan McKee focused on expanding shelter capacity during a 2022-23 crisis where many were unsheltered.

No laws criminalizing homelessness were passed – the state is actually bound by the opposite (the bill of rights).

However, cities like **Cranston** and **Woonsocket** have used police to disband smaller camps, often invoking trespassing on municipal property.

The **RI Coalition to End Homelessness** monitors these and has threatened lawsuits when clear violations occur. - **Conclusion:** Rhode Island stands as a state with a *nominally pro-homeless-rights law* on the books, yet the reality is enforcement still happens.

Advocates like the RI Homelessness Coalition’s Juan Espinoza stress that without enforcement or awareness, the law doesn’t stop authorities from pushing people out of sight【5†L147-L155】【5†L153-L160】.

The trend in RI has been to quietly remove encampments while publicly affirming rights – a bit of a contradictory stance. ## South Carolina - **Columbia Ordinance Revival:** South Carolina passed no state anti-homeless laws, but the capital city **Columbia** in 2023 revived enforcement of an ordinance against “street camping” that had been on the books.

After some backlash (and since shelters were full), the city paused, then resumed enforcement once a few more shelter beds opened. - **Myrtle Beach and Charleston:** **Myrtle Beach** in 2022 began enforcing a new ban on public sleeping in commercial areas during daytime, mainly to move homeless individuals away from tourist-heavy boardwalk spots.

Violators can be fined \$100. **Charleston** continued its practice of not allowing any bedding or personal belongings to accumulate in public parks – city crews swiftly remove unattended items, a policy that discourages encampments from forming.

Charleston police also enforce against aggressive panhandling under a 2016 ordinance. - **State Perspective:** The South Carolina state government has largely left the issue to localities.

Governor Henry McMaster has not made any statements pushing for criminalization; rather, SC has focused on funding more mental health crisis response (which sometimes intersects with homelessness).

In 2023, a budget proviso added funds for emergency shelter beds in Greenville and Columbia, acknowledging needs. - **Greenville Initiatives:** **Greenville**, SC did something notable but not punitive: in 2023 it opened a sanctioned campground area with oversight by a non-profit, as a pilot to get people off unauthorized camps.

Police there cooperated by directing folks to the sanctioned site and warning that other camps would be cleared.

By offering an alternative, Greenville hoped to avoid pure criminal enforcement.

No specific laws were passed addressing homelessness in 2022–2025. **Sioux Falls**, the largest city, has an ordinance prohibiting camping in parks and along the Big Sioux River greenway; police occasionally enforce it, but generally Sioux Falls works closely with its mission shelter to ensure people have a place to go in winter. - **Rapid City** had an unfortunate incident in 2022 with a fire at an unofficial camp in a wooded area.

After that, local officials cleared the area and have maintained a policy of not tolerating camps on public land, but they do so under general trespass laws. - **State Role:** The state government hasn’t been involved.

South Dakota being a rural state with cold climate sees transient homeless often migrate or find indoor shelter (emergency or informal).

In a few cases, law enforcement might give vagrancy citations (South Dakota still has vagrancy statutes) but this is rare.

No legislative debates or proposals of note occurred on this topic. ## Tennessee - **Felony Camping Law (2022):** Tennessee became the **first state to criminalize camping on public property as a felony**.

In 2022, the legislature (led by Republicans) passed House Bill 978, expanding a prior ban on camping on state property to **all public property**【28†L75-L83】【30†L241-L247】.

Effective July 1, 2022, it is a Class E **felony** (punishable by up to 6 years in prison) to camp on city, county, or state-owned land without permission【28†L75-L83】.

The law also made camping along highways or under bridges a misdemeanor with fines and community service【27†L11-L18】.

Tennessee’s move was a dramatic escalation – essentially using the criminal code to threaten prison time for unhoused people sleeping outdoors. - **Additional Measures (2023):** In 2023, Tennessee passed another law (HB 197) requiring the **state Department of Transportation (TDOT)** to coordinate with counties to **remove camps located along highways and under bridges**【28†L79-L87】【28†L201-L209】.

Tennessee has, in effect, doubled down on enforcement as the primary tool. - **Enforcement and Impact:** Law enforcement in Tennessee’s cities have used these laws to increase camp clearances. **Nashville** Metro police began coordinated weekly operations in 2022 to cite or arrest people in prohibited camping areas, in partnership with TDOT for state land.

Several people have been charged under the felony law – though prosecutors have some discretion and there’s scant evidence anyone has been sentenced to years in prison solely for camping.

Usually, an officer will warn, cite for misdemeanor, and only consider felony charges for repeated refusal to vacate.

Nonetheless, the felony threat hangs. **Knoxville, Chattanooga, Memphis** – all have complied, with local police routinely clearing camps and then TDOT or city crews discarding any remaining tents.

Advocates note that by late 2024, **street homelessness had not decreased** in those cities despite the laws; shelters remained full, and many simply moved to more hidden areas.

A study cited by the Vera Institute found that incarceration and forced treatment (as Tennessee seems to favor) are far costlier and less effective than housing【28†L99-L107】【28†L111-L119】.

Still, Tennessee’s approach has been hailed by some conservatives as “making homelessness invisible” – though as one commentator noted, *if locking people up solved homelessness, Tennessee would have ended it by now*【28†L99-L107】【28†L101-L109】. ## Texas - **Statewide Camping Ban (2021, enforced through 2022–25):** Texas implemented a **statewide public camping ban** in 2021 (Senate Bill 1925), which continued to be enforced in the past three years.

This law prohibits camping in public places statewide and makes it a Class C misdemeanor (up to \$500 fine) to do so.

It also directs the state to withhold certain funding from cities that don’t enforce anti-camping laws.

By 2022–2023, Texas was fully into enforcement mode.

The law was drafted from the Cicero Institute model and Texas was the first to pass it, providing a blueprint for other states【13†L33-L38】. - **City Compliance:** **Austin**, which had previously allowed camping, had already reversed course in 2021 via a voter referendum.

Throughout 2022–2025 Austin police and state troopers (occasionally deployed by Gov.

Abbott) have enforced no-camping rules; the state even established a sanctioned camping site (Camp Esperanza) outside Austin to relocate some people. **Dallas** and **Houston** were largely already enforcing local bans – Houston uses a strategy of offering housing then clearing camps under its “Encampment Decommissioning” policy, which ramped up in 2022 with federal funds for housing about 1,000 people.

However, cities like **San Antonio** and **El Paso** reported challenges: after the state ban, more people used hidden areas (drainage ditches, wooded lots), complicating outreach. - **Cicero Influence and Results:** The Texas Public Policy Foundation and Cicero Institute heavily promoted Texas’s law as a success.

Abbott also loudly criticized cities like Austin before the ban and took credit for the crackdown. - **Local Ordinances:** Apart from the state law, **Fort Worth** in 2023 instituted a new ordinance banning solicitation at certain high-traffic intersections after complaints of panhandling – enforceable by fine. **San Antonio** began enforcing a no-camping rule specifically along the tourist-heavy River Walk.

Texas’s large cities are effectively in lockstep with the state’s mandate now, making camping anywhere in Texas a ticketable offense.

That said, Texas has invested some money into alternative strategies (e.g., a 2022 appropriation for building tiny home villages for homeless veterans), showing a mix of approaches. ## Utah - **Enforcement Mandate (2023):** According to the Cicero Institute, **Utah passed a law in 2023 compelling municipalities to enforce their camping bans**【30†L237-L245】【30†L237-L240】.

The exact statute is subtle, but essentially Utah signaled that cities should not adopt policies of non-enforcement.

This came amidst public pressure over visible camps in Salt Lake City. - **Salt Lake City Crackdowns:** In fall 2022 and again in late 2023, Salt Lake City’s Mayor **Erin Mendenhall** ordered **accelerated enforcement against illegal camps** ahead of winter, especially after additional shelter beds opened【25†L29-L35】.

SLC police launched “Operation Rio Grande” style sweeps in the downtown and Rio Grande areas, clearing dozens of camps.

The **Salt Lake City Police Department** even requested more funding for overtime to sustain a homeless camp **“enforcement blitz”** in 2023【25†L5-L13】.

Critics noted that people just move from one spot to another (“whack-a-mole”) and that these actions traumatize those displaced【13†L135-L142】. - **Funding Shifts:** In 2022, the Utah legislature **more than doubled** a **Homeless Shelter Cities Mitigation Fund** (to over \$10 million) to help cities with shelters deal with impacts【26†L225-L234】.

However, *91% of those funds* ended up spent on law enforcement (police and public safety) rather than on social services【26†L231-L239】【26†L232-L239】.

George hired additional police officers with this money【26†L229-L238】.

Advocates like Bill Tibbitts of Crossroads Urban Center argued that using funds for “first responders rather than services” is a sign of policy failure【26†L239-L247】【26†L243-L251】.

Essentially, **Utah’s cities spent state homelessness grants on police** by an overwhelming margin. - **Other Cities:** **Ogden**, UT has been ticketing people for “open-air camping” in city parks and saw individuals like one man, Darrell, accumulate multiple citations【26†L127-L135】【26†L203-L211】.

Police there and in **Provo** offer outreach but also enforce trespass laws if someone refuses shelter.

Spencer Cox, while promoting housing programs, also stated he would *“refuse to let Salt Lake City turn into San Francisco”* regarding street homelessness【25†L39-L47】, indicating a political stance favoring strict enforcement.

In sum, Utah combined increased shelter funding with a clear directive: no unsanctioned camping should be allowed.

This dual approach hasn’t solved homelessness, but it has led to heavy police involvement in managing the issue. ## Vermont - **End of Motel Program:** Vermont’s biggest homelessness-related development was largely administrative but had huge impact: In June 2023, Vermont **ended its pandemic-era motel housing program**, which had provided shelter for over 1,000 individuals experiencing homelessness.

When funding lapsed, many of those people were forced to leave motels with nowhere to go, leading to a sudden spike in unsheltered homelessness in a state that historically had little.

Burlington police and outreach teams removed the encampment, placing some residents in hastily arranged shelters and leaving others to disperse.

This drew protests – Vermont advocates accused the state of effectively *creating* encampments by cutting the motel housing and then criminalizing those who had no alternative. - **Legal Landscape:** Vermont does not have anti-homeless statutes and tends to be progressive.

However, Vermont officials are cautious given constitutional rights.

No formal camping ban law was proposed; instead, Governor Phil Scott’s administration quietly supported more law enforcement at problem sites (like the bus incident camp) while also urging the legislature to allocate more housing aid. - **Outlook:** Vermont’s homelessness problem grew (~500 more people unhoused by late 2023) and its shelter system shrank without the motel program.

But politically, Vermont is more inclined to restore some housing assistance rather than pass laws against camping.

Still, practically, **police have moved people along** from public property when complaints arise, using general trespass laws on state lands and municipal park rules. ## Virginia - **Local Ordinances for Safety:** Virginia saw some municipalities address panhandling as a safety issue. **Roanoke** implemented a ban on standing on road medians under 4 feet wide (2019), and by 2023 police had issued about 100 citations to panhandlers under that law【34†L139-L143】.

Roanoke ramped up enforcement in 2022–23, which they reported as part of traffic safety initiatives. - **Northern Virginia:** In Fairfax County (suburban DC), rather than criminalize, the county launched a program offering stipends to panhandlers to do community clean-up work instead【34†L59-L66】.

This is an incentive approach mirrored in some other cities (like OKC’s program) and indicates a trend to reduce panhandling without outright banning it. **Alexandria, VA** briefly considered an ordinance against camping in certain sensitive areas (after some tents popped up near a Metro station in 2023), but instead opted to increase outreach. - **State Law:** No Virginia state law was passed against homelessness.

The General Assembly did debate issues around *supportive housing* funding, not criminalization.

In fact, in 2021 Virginia had eliminated an old “habitual drunkard” interdiction law used to arrest homeless alcoholics, as it was ruled unconstitutional.

This reflects a state trend away from punitive measures. - **Richmond:** The capital, Richmond, had a visible encampment near the Shockoe bottom area in 2022.

The Richmond police approach has been to use trespass enforcement if a landowner (including city) signs a complaint.

This happened also in **Norfolk** with encampments near the downtown library – Norfolk police issued trespass orders in 2023 to break up a group living there. - **Summary:** Virginia’s approach is mixed: some cities use minor laws to address specific issues (like Roanoke’s median rule, or anti-accosting ordinances in Virginia Beach’s resort strip), but there’s no sweeping crackdown.

Importantly, Virginia Beach in 2022 did heavily enforce existing bans on sleeping on the beach or boardwalk at night to keep the tourist area clear, working with a new homelessness outreach jail diversion program.

This carrot-and-stick approach is more common in Virginia. ## Washington - **Post-SCOTUS Rush of Local Bans:** After the June 2024 Grants Pass ruling, **at least six Washington cities swiftly passed or expanded anti-camping laws**, no longer constrained by the 9th Circuit’s Boise precedent【19†L117-L125】【19†L119-L127】.

These include: - **Aberdeen:** removed prior requirements to offer shelter before camp clearances, and broadened the areas where camping is banned【19†L139-L147】. - **Auburn:** in Sept 2024 amended its camping ordinance to **eliminate the need to offer shelter** and now can require people to leave immediately; violation is a misdemeanor (up to \$1,000 fine and 90 days jail)【19†L147-L155】. - **Kennewick:** similarly removed the shelter offer requirement and set penalties up to \$1,000/90 days, though they still give 72-hour notice to vacate【19†L151-L159】. - **Richland:** strengthened enforcement of its ban (acted in tandem with Kennewick)【19†L153-L161】. - **Lakewood:** passed a brand new law in summer 2024 banning camping citywide; police can clear camps with just 24-hour notice now【19†L159-L167】. - **Spokane:** had already passed a law via Prop 1 (Nov 2023) banning camping within 1,000 feet of schools, parks, and daycares.

After Grants Pass, Spokane began *enforcing* it in August 2024【19†L165-L173】, whereas before they held off due to legal uncertainty.

Spokane Police now actively cite or arrest those camping in prohibited zones, and they also enforce a year-round downtown **sit-lie ordinance** that bars sitting or lying on sidewalks during daytime. - **Lawsuits and State Constitution:** In response, the **ACLU of Washington** filed a lawsuit in August 2024 against Spokane, arguing the anti-camping law violates the *Washington State Constitution’s* ban on cruel punishment【19†L173-L181】【19†L177-L185】.

They contend that criminalizing basic life-sustaining acts is unconstitutional at the state level, even if federal courts allowed it【19†L177-L185】.

However, after 2024, Seattle has been able to enforce with less fear of legal pushback.

The city and King County ramped up sweeps in places like Green Lake and Woodland Park in 2022 and continued through 2023–24.

There’s also a new push by some Seattle council members to enforce against RV “homesteading” (people living in vehicles), possibly by stricter parking enforcement. - **State Government:** Washington State itself, led by Democrats, did not pass anti-homeless legislation – in fact, they’ve invested in programs like the Right of Way Safety Initiative to move people off freeway rights-of-way into housing.

The state did see a failed attempt in 2023 at a bill that would guarantee *some* sanctioned camping areas in large cities (which didn’t pass).

Governor Jay Inslee stayed focused on housing funding.

Thus, Washington’s punitive measures are at the city level, unleashed by the lifting of the Boise constraint but now being tested under state law. ## West Virginia - **Morgantown Ordinance (2024):** Morgantown, home of WVU, passed an ordinance in September 2024 **banning camping and storage of personal property in all public spaces**【20†L1-L9】.

The law set **fines of \$200 for a second offense and \$300 for a third**, and even allowed for jail time for repeat violations【20†L1-L9】【20†L11-L16】.

This was reported as a measure to keep parks safe for general use. - **Other Cities:** **Charleston**, the state capital, has long had a small homeless population.

In 2022 Charleston police started to enforce an old ordinance against sleeping in public parks after dark, mainly to disperse a group that often slept at Magic Island Park. **Huntington** (another city) saw its mayor in 2023 create a task force to address panhandling; rather than ban it outright (likely unconstitutional), they set up a campaign urging residents not to give money on the street but to donate to service providers (the “Real Change, Not Spare Change” initiative), and police enforce littering or public drinking laws at gathering spots. - **State Action:** West Virginia’s state legislature did not pass any specific laws on homelessness.

The focus at state level has been the opioid crisis and economic development.

However, in an interesting twist, in 2023 when the West Virginia University campus area (Morgantown) had an increase in visible homelessness, some legislators discussed the need for *a statewide approach to vagrancy*.

Nothing came of that by 2025. - **Enforcement Culture:** West Virginia’s smaller communities often rely on informal policing – telling someone to “move on” or arresting on unrelated charges (drug possession, etc.) if a person experiencing homelessness is causing concern.

Morgantown’s new ordinance is the most formal criminalization step taken in the state recently, and it likely was influenced by seeing what other states/cities were doing (plus pressure from business owners in the college town). ## Wisconsin - **Proposed Camping Ban (2023):** In early 2023, Republican legislators in Wisconsin introduced a bill to criminalize camping on public property (similar to the model seen in other states).

The bill would have instituted fines and possible jail for camping on state or local government land and aimed to prohibit cities from adopting policies tolerant of encampments.

Madison’s rules prohibit overnight camping in parks without a permit; the city has enforced that since mid-2022, with police and park staff removing tents and referring people to shelters. **Milwaukee** saw a major encampment known as “Camp Hope” near downtown which was cleared in late 2019.

In 2023, Milwaukee joined other cities in an amicus brief to the Supreme Court asking for more leeway to manage encampments【5†L105-L113】【5†L107-L115】, indicating they would enforce more strictly if allowed. - **Governor’s Stance:** Gov.

Evers has focused on housing initiatives, including proposing significant funds for homelessness prevention and affordable units (though the GOP legislature cut some of that).

Thus, no state enforcement mandate exists.

Enforcement is usually a warning, then issuing a trespass citation if ignored.

No trend of issuing lots of tickets was noted – rather, Wisconsin relies heavily on its network of emergency shelters (which, while often full, are expanded in winter under state “no freeze” policies). ## Wyoming - **Minimal Homelessness:** Wyoming has a very small homeless population and did not enact any legislation targeting them.

Cheyenne Police handle any issues on a case basis.

For example, if someone camps in a city park, they’ll be asked to move along or taken to a shelter (the city has a mission and some motel vouchers). - **Jackson Hole** (a wealthy area) had some instances of car/RV dwelling due to high housing costs; the town addressed this by tightening parking ordinances (no overnight parking in certain lots) and enforcing existing vehicle camping bans on public streets. - **State Government:** The topic has not come up in the Wyoming Legislature.

Instead, discussion has centered on lack of affordable housing in energy boom towns, but solutions are around development and social services.

Wyoming’s general approach if an encampment arises (like one appeared outside Casper briefly in 2023) is local law enforcement quietly disperses it citing trespass, often with public sympathy siding with police. - **Conclusion:** Wyoming remains largely untouched by the trend of formal anti-homeless laws simply because the scale of homelessness is so low.

Traditional western state attitudes of local control prevail – with any enforcement done without new laws, under general provisions against trespassing or obstruction if needed. --- **Nationwide Trends:** The above state-by-state review highlights a **clear trend toward increased criminalization and displacement of unhoused people from 2022 through 2025**, particularly in states with conservative leadership.

At least **7 states (TX, MO, TN, GA, OK, FL, KY)** have enacted state laws explicitly targeting camping or homelessness, often drawn from the **same model legislation**【24†L147-L155】【13†L33-L38】.

Several other states saw attempts or proposals along those lines (IN, KS, IA, WI, AZ) that did not become law, at least yet【13†L91-L99】【18†L57-L65】.

Meanwhile, **dozens of cities across both red and blue states tightened local ordinances**, especially after the Supreme Court’s 2024 decision: by late 2024, the National Homelessness Law Center counted **99 new local anti-camping ordinances in 26 states in just a five-month span**【1†L84-L92】.

Common features include **removing “no shelter, no citation” protections**, escalating fines (often starting around \$50–\$100 and rising to \$500 or \$1000)【1†L115-L123】【1†L123-L131】, and even jail time for repeat offenses【1†L139-L142】【19†L119-L127】.

These measures are largely driven by public and political pressure to “clean up” visible homelessness, but advocates uniformly warn that **criminalization does not solve homelessness – it “makes homelessness worse.”**【5†L159-L167】【13†L41-L49】 When people acquire criminal records and fines, it becomes harder for them to secure employment or housing, perpetuating the cycle【5†L159-L167】.

Moreover, displacement often just moves people out of outreach teams’ sight, causing missed connections to housing resources【13†L52-L59】.

The federal government’s stance (under the Biden administration) remains opposed to criminalization, but with localities and states asserting themselves – and now with Supreme Court blessing – the landscape has shifted toward punishment over patience.

Nonetheless, there is pushback: **civil rights organizations (ACLU, NLCHP)** are pursuing state constitutional challenges and highlighting violations of homeless individuals’ rights.

A notable example is the case in Washington state aiming to use the **state’s “cruel punishment” clause** to strike down city bans【19†L177-L185】.

Similarly, states like Rhode Island and Connecticut with Homeless Bills of Rights provide a template (albeit weakly enforced) for a different approach.

Some cities are also experimenting with **alternatives** – such as work programs for panhandlers (Oklahoma City, Fairfax VA)【34†L59-L67】, sanctioned camp areas (Seattle is planning, Las Vegas opened one called “The Courtyard”), and heavy investment in housing (Houston’s 54% reduction in homelessness since 2011 shows housing-first can work).

In summary, **20122–2025** saw a marked **increase in anti-homeless legislation and enforcement** across the country, especially in the South, Midwest, and West.

Whether this trend continues may depend on pending court challenges and the efficacy (or failure) of these policies in reducing homelessness or merely shifting it.

What is clear from the compiled data is that **more jurisdictions than ever are turning to police and penalties** as a response to homelessness – a response advocates say addresses the symptom (visibility) and not the cause (lack of affordable housing, services)【5†L159-L167】. **Sources:** - Local jurisdictions are then pressured to clear encampments rather than provide shelter.

In early 2024, Florida passed a law eliminating long-standing “squatters’ rights.” - Before this change, if someone occupied a property, even without a lease, the owner had to go through a civil court process (unlawful detainer or ejectment) to remove them. - Now, police can remove people immediately without court involvement if the property owner claims they are trespassing. - This bypasses any chance for unhoused individuals to assert defenses (e.g., tenancy disputes, improper notice, bad faith landlord behavior). --- ### 2. **Unlawful Detainer & Ejectment Short-Circuited** - Florida law traditionally required property owners to file for **unlawful detainer** or **ejectment** in civil court before evicting. - These processes ensured notice, hearings, and judicial oversight. - The new laws make those protections irrelevant in cases labeled as “trespassing,” enabling removal by law enforcement on the spot. - This effectively erases due process for unhoused individuals sheltering in vacant properties or lots. --- ### 3. **Expanded Trespass Arrests Without Warning** - By 2022, at least 48 states already had some form of anti-homeless law (such as vagrancy, loitering, or camping bans) on the books【18†L89-L97】.

For example, **Texas** instituted a statewide ban in 2021 making outdoor camping a misdemeanor punishable by a fine (up to $500)【21†L191-L199】, and has since issued over 1,300 citations under that law【21†L197-L202】. **Tennessee** went even further in 2022, becoming the first state to make camping on *any* public land a felony offense【18†L102-L110】.

Other states quickly followed suit: **Missouri** made sleeping on state-owned land a criminal misdemeanor as of Jan. 1, 2023【11†L118-L126】, **Florida** passed a 2024 law banning sleeping or camping in *all* public places statewide【23†L333-L340】, and **Kentucky** criminalized street camping in 2024 (with repeat offenses risking up to 90 days in jail)【30†L297-L304】.

At least **eight states** have enacted these kinds of camping bans at the state level, according to a conservative estimate【30†L269-L275】.

Supreme Court ruling in *Grants Pass v.

Within the year following that decision, **260 new local laws** criminalizing homelessness were enacted in cities around the country (mostly camping bans), and **57 state-level anti-homeless bills** were introduced in 17 state legislatures (8 of which became law)【41†L1-L4】.

In four states – **Texas, Florida, California, and New Hampshire** – sleeping or camping in any public place was effectively outlawed even before these recent moves【18†L89-L97】 (California and Florida had largely relied on widespread local ordinances, whereas Texas and Florida now have explicit state statutes banning public camping【23†L333-L340】【21†L191-L199】).

Violations often carry fines that unhoused individuals cannot pay, leading to warrants, arrest, or jail time.

For instance, Grants Pass, Oregon (whose ordinance was at issue in the 2024 Supreme Court case) imposed **$295 fines** on homeless people using a blanket or cardboard for shelter, and after two citations would ban the person from all city property – so that returning to public space at all meant risking arrest for trespassing【10†L127-L135】【10†L129-L137】.

In Missouri, two or more citations for camping could likewise escalate to trespass charges with **up to 30 days in jail**【10†L129-L137】.

And under Florida’s new law, anyone who refuses to vacate a public area when ordered (even if they have no shelter available) can be **arrested** for unlawful lodging【23†L383-L392】.

Several states have tied the hands of local governments or fast-tracked enforcement in ways that remove traditional checks and balances.

For example, Missouri’s law not only bans public camping statewide, but also authorizes the **state Attorney General to sue cities** that “fail” to enforce the ban or that don’t already have strict local anti-camping ordinances【11†L118-L126】【20†L387-L395】.

In practice, this means a city could be taken to court simply for treating homeless encampments with outreach or leniency instead of punitive sweeps.

Texas lawmakers considered similar penalties: a 2025 Texas bill (since withdrawn) sought to designate any city that didn’t clear camps within 90 days of a citizen complaint as a “**violating local entity**,” allowing the state to intervene and even withhold funding by clawing it back from local tax revenue【21†L177-L185】【21†L179-L187】.

Georgia’s new law likewise *compels* municipalities to enforce anti-camping rules and remove encampments, effectively stripping local discretion【17†L225-L233】.

In New York City, for instance, six unhoused New Yorkers recently filed a federal lawsuit alleging that the city’s aggressive encampment clearances violate their constitutional rights【25†L35-L43】.

The suit documents how city agencies often ignore their own rules requiring a 90-day storage of confiscated items; instead, sanitation workers and police **discard tents, clothing, identification papers, medications, and other essentials on the spot**【25†L39-L47】.

Similar litigation is pending or has been recently settled in cities like **San Francisco, Washington D.C., Los Angeles,** and **San Diego**, all centering on the claim that authorities are trampling on Fourth and Fourteenth Amendment rights by treating homeless people’s belongings as trash.

In San Francisco, a prominent lawsuit by the Coalition on Homelessness alleged the city violated state and federal law by **clearing encampments without offering shelter and by destroying unhoused people’s property** without due process【26†L151-L159】.

That case led to a settlement in 2025 requiring the city to reinforce its “bag and tag” policy – i.e. cataloging and storing any seized belongings – and to pay damages and attorney fees【26†L151-L159】【26†L177-L185】.

Even so, advocates note that compliance is uneven: evidence showed that after initial court orders, San Francisco still ramped up arrests for camping and saw city workers throw most of one man’s possessions into a garbage truck during a sweep, contrary to policy【26†L199-L207】. **Basic due process** – the right to fair notice and an opportunity to be heard before being deprived of property or liberty – is often absent in these scenarios. “If you are an unhoused person, how do you know if it’s a state-owned freeway [where camping is now a crime]?” one Legal Aid attorney asked, highlighting the Kafkaesque ambiguity of Missouri’s law【20†L471-L476】.

Outreach workers in Missouri report that neither police nor service providers received clear guidance on *which* lands are off-limits or how the law will be enforced【20†L397-L405】【20†L451-L460】.

This vagueness means **police have broad discretion** in enforcement, increasing the risk of arbitrary or discriminatory application.

In one especially harrowing incident illustrating the human toll, police in Louisville, Kentucky cited a homeless woman for “unlawful camping” **while she was in active labor**, her clothes soaked from her water breaking【30†L234-L242】.

Bodycam footage captured an officer’s skepticism about her condition (he radioed for an ambulance “just in case I’m wrong” about her going into labor)【30†L242-L249】.

The woman’s baby was born hours later, and her camping charge was eventually dropped in a special court session【30†L238-L247】【30†L251-L259】 – but the incident underscores how these laws can be enforced in callous ways, prioritizing penalization over humanitarian concern. ## Coordinated Efforts and Potential Collusion (RICO Allegations) A striking aspect of the recent crackdown is how **coordinated and systemic** it appears.

One influential player is the **Cicero Institute**, a Texas-based policy group (founded by tech billionaire Joe Lonsdale) that has promoted a template for “Reducing Street Homelessness.” According to a Pew research analysis, in 2021–2022 lawmakers in at least **six states introduced nine bills** closely patterned on Cicero’s model【18†L111-L118】.

Key features of this model legislation include creating state-authorized “sanctioned” camp sites with time limits, **banning all other encampments**, and **penalizing cities** that fail to remove unsanctioned camps【18†L113-L118】.

In *Oklahoma*, legislators passed an anti-camping law in late 2024 and immediately pushed to expand it to cover city and county lands as well【32†L101-L109】.

These examples suggest a **coordinated campaign** to standardize harsher treatment of homelessness, with lawmakers sharing tactics and legislative language across state lines.

At the city level, coordination often occurs through partnerships between **mayors, police departments, and even private entities**.

Many cities now deploy inter-agency “homeless outreach” or “encampment response” teams that include police officers, sanitation workers, and sometimes nonprofit or faith-based groups.

Ostensibly, these teams offer services, but in practice they frequently serve an enforcement role (75% of police-involved homeless outreach teams had a primarily enforcement mandate, one study found)【6†L21-L29】.

In New York City, Mayor Eric Adams made it explicit that his policy was to **“rid the encampments off our street”** and move people out by force if necessary【25†L63-L71】.

Advocates argue these patterns amount to **systemic misconduct**: for instance, the New York lawsuit mentioned earlier claims multiple city agencies have *routinely conspired* to violate homeless individuals’ rights by flouting rules and covering it up【25†L39-L47】.

Such multi-actor involvement, if proven, could potentially meet the definition of an *organized scheme* of wrongdoing. (Indeed, some legal strategists have pondered using **RICO (Racketeer Influenced and Corrupt Organizations) statutes** in creative ways – to treat coordinated, repeated civil rights violations against the homeless as a kind of criminal enterprise.

One federal complaint filed in 2022 alleged a “pattern of racketeering activity” by various officials discriminating against homeless and poor individuals【9†L1-L9】, though courts have been skeptical of applying RICO in this context.) On the legislative front, **governors and state attorneys general** have actively synchronized with local crackdowns.

Texas Governor Greg Abbott, for example, championed the 2021 camping ban after Austin (the state capital) refused to criminalize encampments – effectively wresting homelessness policy from city leaders.

The Florida law explicitly *“aims to end public sleeping”* statewide, despite local concerns that it **“will even criminalize people with nowhere to go”**【23†L343-L350】.

Some state leaders have also worked in concert: e.g. **Tennessee’s governor quietly allowed its felony camping bill to become law without signing**, while cautioning that punishment alone wasn’t a solution – yet no steps were taken to veto or amend the law【14†L19-L27】.

Business improvement districts and downtown associations in cities like Los Angeles, Denver, and Seattle have lobbied for stricter enforcement, at times hiring private security to supplement police patrols in areas with encampments.

Missouri’s law actually **prohibits** the use of state funds for long-term housing like “permanent supportive housing,” mandating that money be diverted to temporary shelters, substance treatment, or mental health programs instead【20†L410-L418】.

This has put service providers in a bind – some Missouri nonprofits feared they might have to **close their doors** or defy either state or federal guidelines, due to the **“seemingly-conflicting” directives** on how funds should be used【20†L415-L423】.

In effect, **state governments are pressuring service providers** to participate in the enforcement paradigm (e.g. running short-term sanctioned camps or treatment programs) rather than pursuing housing-first strategies.

Such collusion between policymakers and certain agencies could be seen as a coordinated effort to prioritize punitive “solutions” even when they conflict with best practices or federal policy. **Federal oversight failures** may also be contributing to the problem.

Department of Housing and Urban Development (HUD) and other federal agencies have generally condemned criminalization of homelessness – calling it counterproductive and even threatening to withhold grants from jurisdictions that criminalize – but **enforcement of these warnings has been lax**【19†L41-L44】.

In 2022, HUD announced a $365 million initiative to address unsheltered homelessness and encampments with housing and services【19†L1-L9】, yet at the same time, states like Texas and Missouri were actively funneling resources away from housing and into policing camps【20†L410-L418】.

There appears to be a disconnect or failure in federal oversight when states can misuse or repurpose federal homelessness funds contrary to HUD’s evidence-based “Housing First” approach.

A vivid example came from the last days of the Trump administration: a July 2020 executive order (titled “Ending Crime and Disorder on America’s Streets”) sought to **reward cities that ramped up anti-vagrancy enforcement** and **punish those that didn’t**, effectively encouraging the criminalization of unhoused people【36†L102-L110】【36†L113-L121】.

It also directed federal agencies to shift funding priorities *away* from “housing first” programs in favor of police-centric and institutionalization strategies【36†L147-L155】.

While that executive order did not outlast the change in administrations, its spirit lives on in many state policies.

The lack of a strong countervailing stance or incentives from current federal authorities means that states and cities face little consequence for enacting these crackdowns – a void that, in the eyes of advocates, constitutes a **failure of federal oversight** to protect civil rights and ensure humane use of funds. ## Constitutional and Legal Challenges The recent anti-homeless measures raise a host of **constitutional issues**, implicating the 4th, 5th, 8th, and 14th Amendments, among others.

Until 2024, one of the strongest legal protections for unhoused individuals was the **Eighth Amendment’s ban on cruel and unusual punishment**.

Federal courts (notably the Ninth Circuit in *Martin v.

However, the Supreme Court’s June 2024 decision reversed that trend: in a 6–3 ruling, the Court upheld the Grants Pass, OR ordinances that banned using **blankets or cardboard for shelter**, reasoning that the laws criminalized conduct, not status, and that the penalties (fines and short jail stints) did not qualify as “cruel and unusual” in the Court’s view【10†L99-L107】【10†L147-L155】.

The majority opinion (by Justice Gorsuch) explicitly stated that federal judges should not be dictating homelessness policy under the Eighth Amendment【10†L105-L113】.

This landmark ruling effectively **removed the Eighth Amendment as a barrier** to camping bans nationwide, emboldening many cities and states to press forward with enforcement even when no shelter is available.

Justice Sotomayor’s dissent argued that the decision **ignored the reality** faced by the unhoused – it “focuses almost exclusively on the needs of local government” and leaves vulnerable people with no choice “either stay awake or be arrested”【10†L111-L119】.

These cases invoke the principle that **poverty does not erase one’s property rights**: even a tarp or a sleeping bag is protected from arbitrary government destruction【35†L173-L181】.

Supreme Court invalidated a vagrancy law in *Papachristou v.

A number of cities have enacted **“sit-lie” ordinances** making it illegal to sit or lie down on sidewalks in business districts; others have “obstruction” rules that in effect make it illegal to **stand still** (or place one’s belongings) on certain public property【23†L353-L360】.

Florida’s new statute, for example, defines prohibited “public lodging” in a way that mirrors these local rules – making it unlawful to “stand, sleep or lie down” in many public rights-of-way【23†L353-L361】.

In other words, a person in a suit napping on a park bench at lunchtime is unlikely to be cited, but an unkempt unhoused person will be – a disparity that raises serious constitutional questions.

It’s worth noting that legal challenges *have* succeeded in many cases despite the uphill battle.

According to the National Homelessness Law Center, as of 2019 about **60% of lawsuits** challenging anti-panhandling or anti-camping laws succeeded, and **77% of challenges** to loitering or vagrancy bans were successful【18†L95-L100】.

In late 2022, a federal magistrate judge in California imposed a preliminary injunction blocking San Francisco from enforcing certain camping bans, citing evidence that the city wasn’t offering shelter and thus enforcement likely violated the Eighth Amendment (this was before the Supreme Court’s *Grants Pass* ruling changed the legal landscape)【26†L183-L191】.

Although that particular protection has narrowed, **state constitutions** and statutes provide another avenue: some state courts interpret their own constitutions to afford greater rights than the federal baseline.

For instance, the Oregon state courts could still find shelter bans violate Oregon’s constitution, even if the U.S.

Constitution is no barrier. **Disability rights laws** are also being invoked – advocates argue that many chronically homeless individuals have disabilities (mental illness, physical impairments) and that blanket crackdowns without accommodations may violate the Americans with Disabilities Act (ADA).

Department of Justice opened an investigation into the **Phoenix Police Department’s treatment of homeless individuals**, examining whether officers engaged in a pattern of violating civil rights or ADA protections during encampment clearings (this echoes patterns seen in other cities)【19†L23-L24】.

While unconventional, the idea is that if multiple actors (say, a city government, a police force, and a private BID security team) conspire to systematically deprive homeless people of their rights through unlawful acts – such as **assaults, theft of property, coercion, or falsifying records of incidents** – those could be considered predicate offenses in a civil RICO case.

A lawsuit in Maine tried this approach, claiming that various agencies and businesses formed a “Maine Fraud Enterprise” that harassed and defrauded a homeless individual, though the case faced significant hurdles【9†L19-L27】【9†L29-L37】.

While no major RICO case has yet succeeded in the homeless-rights context, the very consideration of it underscores how *pervasive and organized* the misconduct has become in some areas.

What used to be viewed as isolated incidents – a cruel cop here, a draconian city ordinance there – now appear as part of an **interconnected pattern** spanning jurisdictions, which is exactly the kind of scenario RICO was designed to address. ## Human Impact: Abuse, Suffering, and Public Health Consequences The enforcement of these anti-homeless laws has led to **widespread human suffering**, documented in news stories, social media videos, and public testimony.

One woman in Sacramento recalled the “humiliation that my city hated me” – she was “denied shelter and yet forbidden to live without shelter,” constantly told by police that merely sleeping outdoors was “against the law”【35†L155-L162】.

This sentiment is echoed by many living on the streets: being rousted by police at night, having one’s few belongings taken, and accumulating court citations create a sense of being *outcast* and persecuted. 【38†embed_image】 *A homeless encampment in San Francisco, California (2022).

Large-scale “sweeps” of such encampments have become common, where city workers and police clear out tents and often dispose of people’s belongings.

Advocates argue that these actions merely displace unhoused residents and destroy their survival gear, without addressing the underlying causes of homelessness.*【18†L59-L67】【18†L122-L130】 The **destruction of property** in sweeps has concrete negative impacts on health and safety.

Losing these can set someone back months in their struggle to get housing or a job.

Louis recorded dozens of calls to shelter hotlines only to be told **every shelter was full**, night after night【20†L369-L377】.

When those unsheltered individuals were later forced out of their tent camps by the new Missouri law, they literally had “**nowhere…to go**” – often ending up hiding in more dangerous spots or losing contact with service providers【20†L379-L387】【20†L423-L430】.

In one illustrative quote, a Missouri advocate noted the law would just “push unhoused people out of sight” so it looks like the problem is solved, “but really they’re just hiding” further from help【20†L423-L430】.

One study found that **criminalization “systematically limit[s] homeless people’s access to services, housing, and jobs,”** trapping them in a cycle of instability【6†L37-L40】.

A recent academic study (published in 2023) confirmed that **encampment bans do not reduce homelessness** overall – in fact, cities that implemented such bans saw no significant decrease in homeless populations compared to cities that didn’t, and in some cases a *rebound increase* in homelessness following sweeps【40†L77-L85】【40†L89-L97】.

The authors noted that any brief drop in visible homelessness (from forcing people to move) tends to be illusory, often followed by a return of encampments or an increase in the number of people living unsheltered, sometimes in neighboring areas【40†L91-L99】.

In short, the evidence suggests these policies **fail to solve homelessness**, and may actually make it harder for people to exit homelessness due to criminal records and loss of belongings【40†L113-L121】.

Even some officials tacitly acknowledge this: in Louisville’s new “homeless court” program, local prosecutors and judges say their goal is *not* to convict people under the camping ban but to direct them to services【30†L253-L261】【30†L255-L263】.

Yet forcing people into court at all – often after a traumatizing police encounter – can undermine trust and create new barriers (many unhoused defendants fear showing up in court, risking warrants).

A public defender in Kentucky criticized the law bluntly: *“this statute was passed because, quite frankly, our legislature doesn’t like to look at homeless people.”*【30†L311-L317】 It “doesn’t make any sense,” he added, noting that courts are left to “do the best with what they’ve been given” by trying to avoid punishment and connect people to aid【30†L307-L315】.

Tensions have grown in cities where housed residents are encouraged to effectively police the unhoused.

In New York, the city set up a hotline for residents to **report homeless encampments** for removal【18†L132-L139】.

Police themselves have been caught on camera using **excessive force** during sweeps: in one video from 2022, NYPD officers were seen **chopping through a makeshift shelter with axes** and tossing a man’s belongings into a garbage truck, as he pleaded that he would leave on his own.

During the COVID-19 pandemic, the CDC explicitly recommended against clearing encampments (unless housing was available) because doing so could accelerate virus transmission by scattering people【40†L144-L152】.

Once an encampment is torn down, homeless individuals may avoid outreach workers, fearing their presence will attract police attention.

Important medications or health records get lost in the shuffle.

And the underlying issues – lack of housing, affordable treatment, and income – remain unaddressed. ## Historical Parallels and Patterns of Discrimination Many observers have noted that today’s anti-homeless crackdowns bear an eerie resemblance to past patterns of systemic abuse in American history. **Laws targeting marginalized groups** under the guise of “public order” are nothing new.

Fast forward to now: modern “quality of life” laws similarly aim to **banish outsiders** (in this case, people without housing) from public visibility.

An Oklahoma state senator, defending a bill to restrict homeless shelters, described her city’s homeless population as *“a vagrant nation”* and the city itself as *“an absolute dumpster fire”* because leaders allowed encampments【32†L126-L134】.

In the Philippines in recent years, President Rodrigo Duterte waged a notorious “war on drugs,” encouraging police and vigilantes to eliminate drug users on sight.

Some homeless advocates fear that the U.S. is seeing a milder version of this mindset applied to homelessness: treating it not as a social issue to solve, but as an enemy to be *forcibly removed*.

It frames the mere existence of unhoused people as an illegal act warranting a state of exception.

In the mid-20th century, American cities often dealt with homelessness (especially for those with mental illness) by confining people in large psychiatric institutions – sometimes subjecting them to abusive treatment or sterilization without consent.

President Trump’s aforementioned executive order flirted with a return to that approach, **encouraging the civil commitment** of homeless individuals en masse “if necessary”【36†L133-L139】.

It is a small step from arguing that homeless people “must be pressured by police to get the services they need” (as the Cicero Institute asserts【30†L269-L272】) to arguing that they should be coerced into institutions for the public good.

Such policies recall some of the darkest chapters of public policy – from the “ugly laws” of the 19th century that banned persons with disabilities or disfigurements from public view, to the eugenics-driven practices that deemed certain populations “unfit” to live freely in society.

In sum, the current pattern of anti-homeless legislation and enforcement is **not an isolated phenomenon** but rather the latest iteration of a historic cycle in which society’s most vulnerable are ostracized and punished.

The **names have changed** – “vagrants” are now “the homeless,” Jim Crow is rebranded as “quality of life” ordinances – but the underlying impulse is the same: to remove an “undesirable” group from sight and to codify a second-class status through law.

Homeless individuals today often feel they are **living under a separate legal system**, one that criminalizes their very existence much as Black people under Jim Crow or others under discriminatory regimes experienced【35†L189-L197】.

This recognition is fueling a growing movement of legal advocates, civil rights organizations, and homeless-led coalitions to fight back and assert that **homelessness is not a crime**.

As one homeless plaintiff in New York stated simply upon filing suit: *“The damaging effects of the sweeps policy represent an abject failure of governance and exacerbate the trend of criminalizing homelessness that has been escalating across the country.”*【25†L55-L63】 There is a call to learn from history rather than repeat it – by dismantling these modern-day vagrancy laws and focusing on housing, not handcuffs, as the solution to homelessness. 【10†L99-L107】【18†L102-L110】【20†L410-L418】【25†L39-L47】【35†L189-L197】【41†L1-L4】 > **User (2025-09-14 19:42:34 UTC):** > > Add all to the canvas document and weave relevant info into the dossier **Assistant (2025-09-14 19:42:37 UTC):** Got it ✅ I’ll pull all of the RICO, constitutional violations, and nationwide impact material I just gathered and **weave it directly into the canvas dossier** as you instructed.

Would you like me to share some specific study references (e.g., meta-analyses or CDC data) so you can read the research directly? > **User (2025-09-30 23:28:03 UTC):** > > If someone is told to leave a house but they not only refuse but break back in when locked out, what crimes have they committed? **Assistant (2025-09-30 23:28:03 UTC):** That situation can lead to **several possible criminal charges**, depending on the exact facts and your local laws.

Here’s how it usually breaks down: --- ## 1️⃣ Trespassing (at minimum) - **Criminal trespass** occurs when someone enters or remains on property after being told to leave. - In most places, once the occupant or owner tells you to leave and you refuse, you are trespassing. - If they lock you out and you force your way back in (even if you once had permission), that’s usually trespass. --- ## 2️⃣ Breaking and Entering / Burglary (if force is used) - **Breaking and entering (B&E):** Entering a property without permission by force (kicking a door, breaking a window, prying a lock). - **Burglary:** In many U.S. states, burglary doesn’t require theft — it’s entering unlawfully with intent to commit any crime inside (like trespass, assault, or theft). - If they break back in after being locked out, that could escalate beyond simple trespass to B&E or burglary, depending on jurisdiction. --- ## 3️⃣ Criminal Damage (if they cause harm to property) - Forcing a lock, breaking a door/window, or damaging anything while re-entering can bring **criminal mischief / vandalism / property damage** charges. --- ## 4️⃣ Possible Assault / Threats (if confrontational) - If they threaten, shove, or use force against the occupant when being told to leave, that could add **assault, battery, or menacing** charges. --- ### ⚖️ Key considerations - **Prior right to be there:** If it’s their own home or they’re on the lease, the situation can be different — “breaking back in” might not be a crime if they have lawful occupancy rights (e.g., a tenant). - **Relationship matters:** Domestic situations (roommates, partners, spouses) can involve extra protections or different eviction processes. --- ✅ **Bottom line:** If someone is clearly not allowed to be there, is told to leave, refuses, and forces entry again, **criminal trespass** and likely **breaking and entering/burglary** (plus any property damage) are on the table.

If there’s force against people, **assault** could also apply. ⚠️ Laws vary by state/country.

If this is about a real incident, it’s safest to: - Call local police if there’s an immediate threat or forced entry. - Consult a lawyer to understand property/tenant rights in your jurisdiction.

Here's the legal breakdown: --- ### 📕 **1.

Constitution)** - **Unlawful Seizure:** Seizing property without a warrant or court order violates your constitutional right to be secure in your “persons, houses, papers, and effects.” - **Destruction of Property:** When officers knowingly destroy belongings without legal justification, it constitutes an unlawful seizure (see *Soldal v.

Cook County*, 506 U.S. 56 (1992)). --- ### 📗 **2.

Florida State Law** - **Fla.

Law enforcement has no power to destroy property absent exigency or lawful warrant. - **Conversion (civil tort):** Taking or destroying someone's property unlawfully constitutes conversion—a tort allowing for compensatory and punitive damages. --- ### 📘 **3.

Civil Rights Violations (42 U.S.C. §1983)** - If the destruction was done “under color of state law” (e.g., officers participating or standing by), it forms grounds for a federal civil rights lawsuit. - **Case Law Support:** - *Lavan v.

City of Los Angeles*, 693 F.3d 1022 (9th Cir. 2012): City violated Fourth and Fourteenth Amendments when police seized and destroyed homeless persons’ property without notice. --- ### 📙 **4.

Disability Rights & Equal Protection** - If any of the seized property included medical items, assistive devices, or identification necessary for benefits, it may also violate: - **ADA Title II** (denial of access through discriminatory enforcement) - **Fourteenth Amendment** (discriminatory deprivation of property without equal protection) --- ### 🟥 **Conclusion** The coordinated destruction and loss of your property: - Violates constitutional protections - Exceeds law enforcement authority under Florida law - May qualify as **intentional state-sponsored deprivation of civil rights**, actionable in federal court Would you like this codified into your timeline or expanded into a formal civil complaint draft? > **User (2025-06-18 20:11:21 UTC):** > > Phase 1: gay was also guilty of tampering with evidence.
